<!-- target: Section 3 -->
### From the primary corpus: trades per session, the fourth-trade rule, and bias

**Trades per session.** The per-session cap was explicit in spring 2026 and is tied to the bias being wrong: ("Three to four trades max. You don't want to over risk into a situation that's not playing out in your favor. If you're winning, then you can keep trading obviously. If you're losing, it's not the best to continue because that usually means your bias isn't playing out and then you should stop." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~3:02, L132; `stops_psychology.json`). The fourth trade is conditional: ("I stop trading at 11:00 or after three trades usually. Sometimes a fourth trade only if it's like an A+ setup, but yeah, it's a bit too late for continuation if that makes sense." — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~4:54, L193; `stops_psychology.json`). On a day where everything aligned he took one: ("So, everything lined up, so I just took one trade today" — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~28:07, L961). In June a three-loss streak on one account just moves him to the next account: ("If I lose three in a row, I don't really care. I'm just going to trade the next account" — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~5:39, L225; `stops_psychology.json`), and by August losing streaks are accepted as the price of the bias: ("I know I have a bias towards a fair price. If I stop buying and I only lose three times, or only lose once or twice," — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~2:21, L122); ("it could be entirely possible that I might have three or even four losses in a single day before I manage to get the price to reverse." — JJ, 4IGbxmKJ4BU ~4:47, L233). In September the session is one continuation and three to four reversions ("And then after that I'll take usually like three to four trades trying to revert that move." — JJ, -lxNWJGWtbg ~0:26, L70). In October the three-loss stop is explicit for reversions: ("Personally, if I'm trying to trade reversion and I lose three in a row, then I'm done. It's just a market that's most likely not going to revert according to my statistics." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~34:24, L1124; `stops_psychology.json`); ("Now all I'm going to do is try to revert towards the pre-news price and again stop after three losses." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:10:50, L2238; `backtests_news.json`); and a 2 p.m. session that month ran two continuations and three reversions ("Then no more continuations for me personally. Reversion one, reversion two, reversion three. So one, two, three wins, two losses for that session." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~29:57, L922; `fair_value_sessions.json`). The whole-operation count is 10 to 20 signals a day: ("The entry criterion is literally just one candlestick movement. I'm being extremely aggressive. I am looking to complete at least 10 transactions per day during the morning in New York." — JJ, KN7j6NXXAio ~2:02, L111); ("I have biased entries and take 10 to 20 per day." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~20:37, L710). The per-account count is one: ("I also do one transaction per day on each of my accounts, just to make it easy." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~27:35, L940; `stops_psychology.json`); ("my goal right now is trade every account once a day, exactly." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~18:22, L720; `eval_risk_math.json`). The quality threshold is set by the account count, in his own words: ("If you have fewer accounts, you could obviously choose better configurations. I try to go through a lot of accounts every day, so I use slightly lower quality settings." — JJ, 0uAQUHEB_L8 ~21:48, L809); ("Looking back, don't take that because the original unfair move never happened. So there's like there's no reason you should ever take that. I would take it because I have a ton of accounts to take." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~27:18, L897). The open continuation is where the cheapest accounts go, fired seconds apart: ("Continuations are the fastest ones, like they'll finish, they'll win the fastest or lose the fastest. You could use your least worthy accounts." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~14:27, L572); ("there's no reason why you couldn't send one, wait 5 seconds, then send another, and then get through almost like five evals in a minute or two on the open continuation." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~14:35, L577); and within an evaluation the strongest triggers are saved for the last day ("So, the break of structures and the more confident reversions with the third day, continuations, those really quick scalping ones in and out would be better for like the first second day sort of thing, but third day just save it for the best ones." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~12:10, L486).

**Bias.** The one-line model: ("I trade high time frame mean reversion, low time frame continuation." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~2:31, L115); ("the strategy I operate will utilize a continuation of the session opening, which means capitalizing on an unfair move with a volume entry, and then taking multiple reversals towards my personal bias." — JJ, 0uAQUHEB_L8 ~16:08, L607). The higher-time-frame bias is always the inverse of the recent move: ("high time frame bias is just for reversion. So pretty much just inverting whatever happened for the previous 6 to 12 hours." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~4:11, L200); ("we had this large rise all night. So, usually I'm seeing a high time frame reversion because it's been climbing all night. So, my high time frame bias was reversion." — JJ, RnwOqmEF6Rk ~4:24, L189); ("the high time frame bias is bullish because it fell overnight. So I definitely wouldn't even go short." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~6:24, L241); ("There is a structural break and a displacement candlestick here, and the longer timeframe bias is short to reverse the move overnight" — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~18:36, L645). Overnight that bias is the whole trade: ("virtually all of my overnight trades will be reversals to the AM price in New York. It's basically just high timescale reversal." — JJ, MIWsdldzJgQ ~5:41, L218). The bias is read on the 5-minute chart or by looking back on the 1-minute, with a 30-60 minute horizon: ("Everything here is 1 minute. The only thing I'll do on the 5 minutes is look for my high time frame bias." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~5:59, L255; `fair_value_sessions.json`); ("So I have a high time frame bias of where the price is going to charge in for the next 30 minutes to an hour." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~11:06, L446; `fair_value_sessions.json`). Gaps are the shorthand: ("As soon as 6 p.m. arrives, you have a slight bias in your favor towards closing the gap." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~4:17, L171); ("the gap is a very easy way to mark the bias on higher timeframes." — JJ, KhooqEQK9bA ~12:26, L526; `fair_value_sessions.json`). The low-time-frame bias is the opening candle, which at a daytime open (the 2 p.m. session in g68HHbrIBBg) may be taken against the higher-time-frame bias ("It goes against the short bias in high timeframes, but it was a structural break. Good enough." — JJ, g68HHbrIBBg ~4:31, L192) and may even outweigh it when strong ("Okay, green candle. So, 100% long bias today. Like very very very very strong. Um we took alltime highs in the morning. So, this is the alltime high. So, first of all, we're very close to that. Second of all, the opening candle is green. Third of all, news is green." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~12:48, L365), but at 6 p.m. two opposing factors veto the continuation ("I'm also not going to take continuation shorts because the opening candle is green and the higher time frame bias is green because 9:30 of that morning is above. Uh so that is too many things that I'd be going against for my continuation trade." — JJ, BLvsYJ4sqn8 ~1:26, L76). The September statistical framing: the bias is a slight linear drift that makes the entry almost irrelevant: ("You're expecting linear, very, very slight linear drift from the current point in time. No matter where you're entering, even if you're entering here, you're still expecting a slight linear drift." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~4:22, L178); ("The entries can add a very slight amount of edge, 0.1%. It is always worth adding edge if you're able to, which is why I trade this specific entry model of break of structure entries just because it adds 0.1% edge." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~5:36, L220); ("I just assume that a slight linear drift in the direction of my bias and then I can put in a prof from account trade on any point in my bias. I don't need a very specific entry criteria" — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~2:20, L107).

**The 1:4 and tight-structure variants (from the risk and sessions files).** Structure-break entries carry a small stop, which is where his 1:4 trades come from: ("So, the good thing about these sort of trades though is you're risking like so few points. Your RR is really good, so I believe I was in this sort of trade uh 1:4" — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~10:45, L384; `risk_sizing.json`); ("my stop loss is usually quite low in these structure breakout trades." — JJ, 4IGbxmKJ4BU ~7:55, L367); at all-time highs ("there are so many points in my favor like this 100 points exactly and you can have such small stops like 25 points and you just have to win a little bit more than like 20% to be profitable." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~46:04, L1247). The ratio is chosen per account: ("Just because the risk reward on eval I do 1 to 1.5. Funded sometimes 1 to 2, 1 to 3, 1 to 4, 1 to 5, whatever." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~16:56, L588; `risk_sizing.json`); ("If I'm taking like a one to four risk-to-reward, then my bias only needs to be right about 20% of the time." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~0:45, L95; `risk_sizing.json`); never on a consistency account ("with a 1 to four on a consistency account you should never do that." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~44:09, L1422; `risk_sizing.json`); and it fails in chop ("it's too choppy to where my one to four trades that need like huge extensions are not really hitting very well" — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~11:24, L392; `risk_sizing.json`).

**Evolution.** March-April 2026: three trades a session, a fourth only for an A+, stop when the bias is not playing out. June: 15-minute continuation window; three losses in a row move him to the next account. July-August: at least 10 signals in the NY morning, 10-20 a day, one trade per account per day, overnight limit orders at several levels, losing streaks accepted. September-October: one continuation plus three to four reversions per session, layering one account per break, bias read as a 6-12-hour inversion, and three reversion losses in a row end the session. The October statements are current; the October three-loss stop restates the spring per-session cap in a narrower form.

**Contradictions.** (a) "If you're losing, it's not the best to continue" (Mar) against "If I stop buying and I only lose three times" (Aug) against "if I'm trying to trade reversion and I lose three in a row, then I'm done" (Oct, KHEQ5g55dQ4 L1124, stops_psychology.json); the October statement is the latest and restores a three-loss stop for reversions. (b) "I only like to take continuations in the opening candle direction" regardless of HTF (June, Aug) against "assuming it aligns with a high time frame bias" (Oct). (c) The dossier's cadence row ("around 10 trades per day" in the $1.6M video) understates it: the video says at least 10 in the NY morning alone. (d) The dossier's Section 10 item 20 is not a contradiction: his grading and "every displacement" coexist by account type. (e) Section 6's consecutive-loss rule (three in a row end the session), reported there second-hand from the Chart Fanatics episode, has verbatim support in that episode's transcript (KHEQ5g55dQ4 L1124, stops_psychology.json), limited to reversions; the March "Three to four trades max" and the April fourth-trade rule are the per-session cap it narrows.

## 4. Risk model

| Rule | Detail | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Stop by volatility | Session-opening candle body over 25 points: 50-point stop, 76-point target, contracts halved; otherwise 25-point stop, 38-point target ("if the candle's over 25 points, then I don't do my 25 stop 38 profit. I would divide size by two and I'll do 50 and 76"; "Never less than 25 points in the New York session"). The 1-minute ATR ladder (above 20: 50; 7-20: 25; below 7: 16.5) is the fxreplay presenter's own construction ("7 to 20 was kind of the range that I was, uh, testing and playing around with"); no JJ statement mentions an ATR threshold or a 16.5-point stop | **JJ** (UVKVSWKFlvo L93; KHEQ5g55dQ4 L215, L2271); ATR ladder **3rd** (SNO1wqJTq5A L350-361) |
| Size by bracket | Contracts keep the dollar risk constant: one NQ contract on 25/38 is "-500 plus 760"; the 50/76 bracket runs at half the contracts ("But what you should do is you cut your contract in half."); "in a 50K account you get two contracts and in a 150K account you get a maximum of three contracts". The 1 / 2 / 3-contract, about-$1,000-per-tier ladder is the fxreplay presenter's | **JJ** (KHEQ5g55dQ4 L2147, L2161; 4BXpI-hYqe0 L294); ladder **3rd** (SNO1wqJTq5A L380) |
| Target | 1:1.5 on evaluations ("Evaluations always going to be a 1 to 1.5. It's going to be 2538 or change the contract size to make it 5076."); consistency-rule funded accounts 1:1 to 1:1.5, sometimes 1:2, never 1:4 ("I'll take a one to 1.5 risk-to-reward on consistency accounts"; "with a 1 to four on a consistency account you should never do that."); funded accounts without a consistency rule 100-point targets and 1:3 up to 1:6 ("I'm going to choose to take the 100 point trades on funded accounts that don't have consistency."), with the stop shown as 25 on one account (gjWiGBiVis0 L475) and 50 on another (7KJOh2NF-lQ L999); funded targets come in 25-point steps (75, 100, 150, 175, 200) from his dashboard | **JJ** (KHEQ5g55dQ4 L366, L418, L1422, L336, L425, L1852, L1930) |
| Losing weeks | Week-13 video description: risk management "to ensure payouts even during losing weeks"; in the Ep. 2 transcript he says it himself: "The candle body is 33 points, which means I will size my contracts in half, which gives me a 50-point stop and a 76-point take profit" and "This one was actually 50 point stop on both of these trades just because of how much volume there was. Again, 157 point candle." (xTxrtFKlQfc L94, L232) | **JJ** description / **3rd** paraphrase |
| ATR period | not published; code uses Wilder 14 | assumption |

**Static risk, in his words (Titans of Tomorrow clip).** "The stop loss
take profit are static ... on prop firms, it is infinitely better to use
static risk in terms of like exactly 1,000, exactly 500." Asked whether a
structure-based stop would be better: "No. No. I found infinitely better to
have a static stop loss." On management: "I never go break even ... unless
there is a new session opening ... Or, if we have news coming out ... like
95% of my trades I'm not going to go break even. I find it better to just
let it play out." And the thesis in one line: "a small bias is like
extremely profitable on prop firms if you have good risk management."

**His own reasoning for 1.5R (JJ, `risk-model` angle, 38 facts).**
Evaluations are "minus 2K max loss and plus 3K profit target, making it
automatically optimal to do a one to 1.5" (sozai transcription; verbatim: "This in and of itself is a 1 to 1.5. So if you separate your trades into a 1 to 1.5, you're automatically going to have a higher pass rate just because of how the draw down trails", KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~8:22, L328, 2026-10-04; first stated 2026-03-28, 3L8xdh3oPm4 L446); trading in 1:1.5 units raises
the pass rate "based on how the drawdown trails"; break-even is 40%; his
sample ran 57.5% (80 trades over 8 days, KN7j6NXXAio L47); his later stated edge is lower: "my specific advantage is a 41% success rate. 41% with a risk-benefit ratio of 1.5" (2BtwoTCxcbc, 2026-09-03, L379; also 0uAQUHEB_L8 L687) and "My win rate is much lower than this in reality when I'm doing 1.5R trades" (BLvsYJ4sqn8 L806). Posture: "aggressive risk to get funded and then
conservative risk management to stay funded" (summary wording; verbatim: "I'm really aggressive on the evals just to pass them super quick. That way I can decrease the months to payout", XhsbfEdJBAc ~12:13, L452, and "I trade the funded slower, which slightly increases that, but it has the payout rate going up more than the months to payout going down would impact me", L457); "on the eval, optimize for
your pass rate; on the funded, maximize your expected value: probability
of getting a payout multiplied by how large that payout is". The sizing
exception is his own, stated in at least eleven videos: "My usual rule for that is if the candle's over 25 points, then I don't do my 25 stop 38 profit. I would divide size by two and I'll do 50 and 76." (UVKVSWKFlvo L93; also KHEQ5g55dQ4 L215, KN7j6NXXAio L268, xTxrtFKlQfc L94, 7KJOh2NF-lQ L468, 74CRg-mID5c L862, g68HHbrIBBg L504); he measures the candle body, applies it to the continuation off each session's opening candle, and calls the two brackets "more or less the same" (74CRg-mID5c L1301). The business math he teaches:
cost to funded = fee / pass rate ($100 / 0.30 = $333); cost per dollar of
drawdown = cost to funded / drawdown ($250 / (1/3) = $750 for $4,500 = 16.6 cents per dollar; the $750 is the average funding cost, not the fee); EV of an evaluation =
P(payout) x payout - P(no payout) x fee (10% x $2,000 - 90% x $100 =
+$110, his July 2026 weighting; by 2026-09-29 he subtracts the fee in full: "Multiply the probability of the upside by how much the upside is and then you subtract your fee" (nvOaN6mRu-o ~6:34, L209), so with a gross $2,000 payout
the EV is +$100); the $1,800-per-day optimal target belongs to a student's account, not his ("All of his earnings are $1,800. Or pretty close to it. It should be $1,800.", AxP-cg50TdM L324); his own funded boxes are "risk 1,000, go for 2,000", "risk 1,000, go for 1,500", "risk 500, go for 2,000" (aCOgfvL6lK8 L577) and on E8 "my risk was 3,000 per trade" against a 15,000 target (AxP-cg50TdM L399);
about 40 accounts "where all of your accounts end the day traded" is six
figures a month; he says he spent $550,000 on evaluations over 19 months to September 2026 (dJdBnSBJlgQ ~3:04-3:34, L117-L131: about 2,200 evaluations at an assumed $250 average, 116 a month across about 20 firms) and, on 2026-10-04, "probably 620,000 maybe a bit less" against $2M of payouts, "more than 3x return on my investment" (PN1UKQMPb5M ~16:17, L515).
Variance he reports: -$21,000 over seven days inside a +$48K month with a
$45K payout. `python -m fpt.cli evalmath` reproduces the arithmetic.

**Reported second-hand from the Chart Fanatics episode** (KHEQ5g55dQ4,
uploaded 2026-10-04; the reading of the GB10 session that holds the
transcript, see `docs/research/chartfanatics-KHEQ5g55dQ4-notes.md`; every
item was to be checked against the transcript before promotion; the machine-verified extraction now confirms in his own words the 1:1.5 evaluation ratio (L328, L741), the 50k example (L328), cost to funded = price / pass rate (L800) and the risk-of-ruin passage (L2427-L2452: 0.30 x 0.30 = 9%, 91% complement, 0.91^10); the 100-point funded target, the opening-range halving and the per-session three-loss stop are still to be checked before they are promoted to his
own words): a 25-point stop and 38-point target (verified verbatim: "Evaluations always going to be a 1 to 1.5. It's going to be 2538 or change the contract size to make it 5076.", L366); contracts halved when the opening candle exceeds 25 points (verified: "If the opening candle is larger than 25 points I'll just simplify it. Cut my contracts in half.", L215); 1:1.5 on evaluations and consistency-rule accounts, sometimes 1:2 and never 1:4 there (verified, L418, L1422), 100-point targets and 1:3 to 1:6 on funded accounts without a consistency rule (verified, L336, L425; `--target-points`; the stop he pairs with 100 is 25 in gjWiGBiVis0 L475 and 50 in 7KJOh2NF-lQ L999, by account); the 50k example of a $2,000 drawdown against a $3,000 target; 45
accounts, never broken down by firm; and cost to funded = evaluation price
divided by pass rate.

**The arithmetic the method rests on.** At the third-party-measured 52-54%
win rate and 1.5R the expectancy is +0.3 to +0.35R per qualifying trade;
the breakeven win rate is 40%. Full Kelly on the drawdown allowance is
about 23%; $1,000 on a $3,000 Topstep drawdown is 33%, i.e. more than
Kelly. That per-trade Kelly reading is the replication's, not his: he used the Kelly criterion only to size how many evaluations a bankroll should buy ("buy this eval because it cost 50, this one because it cost 80, this one cost 100", aCOgfvL6lK8 ~59:02, L2061) and no longer uses it (L2078); his own reason for many accounts is that expected value "is generated through getting new funded accounts because of the cost, not growing your funded accounts in profit" (dJdBnSBJlgQ ~14:48, L450).
`python -m fpt.cli edge` reproduces these numbers; `python -m fpt.cli
evaluation` shows the pass-probability curve versus fixed risk per trade
under the preset's own drawdown rule. For Topstep 100k (EOD-trailing limit
locked at the start balance, $2,000 soft daily limit) at a 54% / 1.5R edge
and 1.5 qualifying trades a day it peaks near 20-30% of the drawdown at a
73-75% pass probability (4,000 sims); a static-drawdown scan would say 80%,
which is why the firm's rule matters.

**Calibration warning.** Ten trades a day at 54% / 1.5R would be +3.5R per
account per day. His own reported results imply far less: "$105,700 in 3
weeks" across ~40 accounts is about $176 per account per trading day,
~0.18R of payout at $1,000 risk. At 0.35R expectancy per trade that is what
about 0.5 R-producing trades per account per day would yield if payouts
equalled P&L; payouts are net of splits, caps, breaches and unfunded
accounts, and the third-party backtests log about 1.2 qualifying signals a
day (Section 8). The simulators default to 1.5 qualifying signals per day
per account in copy mode, an upper bound on his cadence rather than a fit
to his payouts.

<!-- target: Section 4 -->
### From the primary corpus: stops and targets

**1. Rules in his own words**

*1.1 Static distances, never structure.* The stop and the target are fixed point or dollar distances chosen for the account's rule set, not chart levels: ("Each stop loss I use is static. I'm not going to put my stop loss at the previous low or above the previous high because that's what many strategies do. But that's not what the drawdown of a funding account does" — JJ, PMGhHCjYG5c ~17:08, L724); ("I just target a specific amount of points because that's how I backtested." — JJ, RnwOqmEF6Rk ~3:25, L157); ("on prop firms, it is infinitely better to use static risk in terms of like exactly 1,000, exactly 500." — JJ, -lxNWJGWtbg ~7:19, L306); ("You should absolutely not have a discretionary stop loss. Each one of them should be fixed. You must link it to the mathematics of the evaluation according to your strategy and success rate" — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~6:44, L291). What varies is the contract count: ("And with the stop-loss, I only change the contract size. My take profit and stop-loss are static." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~13:27, L473). "Risk management" is the triple: ("whenever I say risk management, I will mean your stop-loss, your profit target, and your contract sizing." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~2:54, L127). One target, no scaling out: ("Leaving runners and partials is probably one of the worst things you can do on prop firms." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~6:46, L300).

*1.2 The evaluation bracket: 25-point stop, 38-point target.* ("I'm just going to have all of them be 38 points TP, 25 points stop loss, and then you can choose your contract sizing based on your risk aversion." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~12:56, L459); ("So I'll just use a 3825. That's what my programmed backtest has indicated as the most cost-effective for a 1 to 1.5 risk-reward ratio." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~2:28, L111); ("I usually, or actually always, use 3825 for my valuation operations. It's a 1:1.5, and it's the best way to pass the evaluations of funding companies." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~1:13, L70); ("Evaluations always going to be a 1 to 1.5. It's going to be 2538 or change the contract size to make it 5076." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~9:37, L366). Captions write the pair as "3825", "2538", "38.25" or "25.38"; all mean 38 target over 25 stop. In dollars on one NQ contract: ("So if I'm doing 25 and 38 with one contract of NQ features, this is -500 plus 760." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:07:52, L2147). The 76 (not 75) in the doubled bracket is a commission allowance: ("I do 76 instead of 75 because there are commissions and fees. So that basically makes it a 75." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~15:35, L543). The 25-point stop is also the modal output of his optimiser: ("the most common stop losses that I end up seeing from my optimization is 25 points." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:00:56, L1935), with the alternatives ("37.5, 50, and 75. So, a few different options, but again, you have to optimize it for your specific account." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:01:04, L1939). Floor in the morning session: ("Never less than 25 points in the New York session." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:11:55, L2271). Session dependence: ("But I usually do 25 points stop especially on the less volatile sessions. New York sometimes I'll do 50 but for these less volatile ones it's easier to do 25." — JJ, EyR3i2GxwyA ~2:00, L96).

*1.3 The opening-candle rule: over 25 points, double the bracket and halve the contracts.* This is the only volatility adjustment he states in his own words, and he states it in at least ten videos. ("The candle is 29 points, so I would do a 50 point stop and a 76 point take profit. My usual rule for that is if the candle's over 25 points, then I don't do my 25 stop 38 profit. I would divide size by two and I'll do 50 and 76." — JJ, UVKVSWKFlvo ~1:56, L93); ("If the opening candle is larger than 25 points I'll just simplify it. Cut my contracts in half. That would make this 76 and that would make this 50." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~4:41, L215); ("if the opening candle is over 25 points, I'll use a 5076 instead of a 2538. So that's a small ATR adjustment you can change." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~13:18, L468); ("opening candle, 23.5 points. Remember, 25 is our benchmark that determines when we will use more or less volume." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~25:09, L862). What is measured is the candle body, in the two videos where he says which: ("The body of the opening candle is 18 points, so risk management says, "Go with a 25-point stop, 38-point take profit."" — JJ, UVKVSWKFlvo ~6:55, L258); ("The candle body is 33 points, which means I will size my contracts in half, which gives me a 50-point stop and a 76-point take profit" — JJ, xTxrtFKlQfc ~1:57, L94). The boundary is inclusive in one video (("50 point stop loss because the candle was 25 points or more and a 76 point take profit for my standard 1 to 1.5 risk to reward trade." — JJ, EyR3i2GxwyA ~0:44, L54)) and a 26-point candle still qualifies (("It is 26 points. So I would still do the 0.5." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~18:59, L529)). The rule is per session, not per day: the 18:00 Asian opening candle triggers it too (("Asia opened with the biggest red candle I've ever seen, 32 points, was go short with a 5075-76." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~15:35, L543); ("Opening candle was 40 points, so that means I will use 50 point stop, 76 point take profit." — JJ, EyR3i2GxwyA ~1:22, L75)). It applies to the continuation off that candle; in the backtest video reversions keep one contract: ("I'll only do the half contract on opening opening one." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~2:07, L82). He treats the two brackets as equivalent in expectation: ("Sometimes I'll do 76-50. It's more or less the same." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~38:25, L1301); ("stop losses don't really matter on prop firms. It's all the same EV. The profitari is where the EV is generated or lost." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:28:52, L2789) (the caption's "profitari" is "profit target").

*1.4 Wider stops on reversions with a lot of room or volume (discretionary).* ("Now I know I usually say 2538. There's a little discretion you can add. In this case you have 243 points in your favor. If it's over 100, then I would probably give in and place the stop at 50 points just because the volume is extremely high that day." — JJ, CzxSgYujDxs ~9:05, L319; medium confidence); ("in this one, I did use a 50-point stop loss just because there was 150 points in my favor, so I sized down on this trade." — JJ, xTxrtFKlQfc ~4:01, L163); ("This one was actually 50 point stop on both of these trades just because of how much volume there was. Again, 157 point candle." — JJ, xTxrtFKlQfc ~6:05, L232). Entry location matters: ("if I enter at the top of these then I want to have a larger stop loss because I assume some part of retracement is normal. If I'm entering down here it's fine to have a lower smaller stop loss." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~24:51, L770).

*1.5 No management, with named exceptions.* ("I never go break even, which might be interesting to hear, unless there is a new session opening. Like, if I'm in a trade at 6:00 p.m. and then Asian session opens, I will go break even." — JJ, -lxNWJGWtbg ~8:50, L358); ("Or, if we have news coming out, then I will go break even. But, like 95% of my trades I'm not going to go break even." — JJ, -lxNWJGWtbg ~8:57, L362); ("I do recommend going break even if the market's going to open while you're in a trade, just because you have crazy wicks like this, just filled with the overnight volume." — JJ, UVKVSWKFlvo ~4:41, L184). A rarer third exception is a structure break against a stalled continuation: ("I'm not usually a fan of going to the break-even point unless it's absolutely necessary. In this case, it is. The reason for that is that the initial unfair continuation basically stops and breaks the structure against you" — JJ, KN7j6NXXAio ~16:39, L637); ("I set this stop to break even just because if it breaks structure down, then it's probably cooked." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~13:36, L435). The 50%-to-target break-even rule in the "40 Trades" backtest is the host's own: ("At this point, I would move my stops to break even, meaning I have accomplished half of the space here. So, I do tend to move my stops break even that way." — host, c61c4CxTpYI ~9:08, L240).

**3. Evolution**

- 2026-03-28 to 2026-04-03 (3L8xdh3oPm4, Lep139B5EbU): 25/38 is already "what I talk about usually".
- 2026-06-01 to 2026-06-21 (UVKVSWKFlvo, MVP7X-3v8xk, xTxrtFKlQfc): the opening-candle rule is "my usual rule"; the body is the measure; half contract only on the opening trade; a 50 stop on reversions when room or volume is large.
- 2026-07-20 (7KJOh2NF-lQ): the bracket is attributed to "my programmed backtest"; the rule is called "a small ATR adjustment"; 76 instead of 75 for commissions.
- 2026-08-23 (74CRg-mID5c): 25 is "our benchmark"; the two brackets are "more or less the same".
- 2026-09-06 (KhooqEQK9bA): one slip to a 20-point stop (~6:21 L283), otherwise 25/38.
- 2026-10-04 (KHEQ5g55dQ4, PN1UKQMPb5M; latest): everything above restated, plus the 25-point floor in New York and the 25 / 37.5 / 50 / 75 optimiser set. Nothing retracts the June-August statements; the October rule set is current.

**4. Contradictions**

- Threshold strictness: "over 25" and "larger than 25" (UVKVSWKFlvo, KHEQ5g55dQ4, KN7j6NXXAio) against "25 points or more" (EyR3i2GxwyA ~0:44 L54). No example sits exactly at 25.
- What to do when the candle is big: ("In the opening candle, if it is greater than 25 points, you can reduce the size, but 3825 will be fine." — JJ, 0uAQUHEB_L8 ~17:02, L639) against ("then I don't do my 25 stop 38 profit. I would divide size by two and I'll do 50 and 76." — JJ, UVKVSWKFlvo ~1:56, L93). He also calls the two brackets interchangeable, so the disagreement is about variance, not expectancy.
- Stop size in the evaluation: ("For me, that static number is 38 points of take profit and 20 points of stop loss in the evaluation." — JJ, KhooqEQK9bA ~6:21, L283) against ("Here you get another good trade of 38 points with a stop of 25 points." — JJ, KhooqEQK9bA ~8:00, L349) in the same video and 25 everywhere else; treat 20 as a slip.
- Do stops matter: ("stop losses don't really matter on prop firms. It's all the same EV." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:28:52, L2789) and ("A different risk isn't going to change much. The reward is where everything changes." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~6:56, L259) against ("I've lost far more accounts due to poor sizing than due to bad operations." — JJ, PMGhHCjYG5c ~14:02, L598) and ("And when you're in a trade with a clear trend, the higher your stop loss, the more likely you are to profit from it" — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~7:33, L322).
- Against the current dossier: Section 4 presents a 1-minute ATR ladder (50/25/16.5) as the stop rule and the opening-candle rule as the fxreplay presenter's wording. The corpus shows the reverse: the opening-candle rule is his, stated repeatedly; the ATR ladder and the 16.5-point stop never appear in his words (see the next block), and the 16.5 tier conflicts with his "never less than 25 points in the New York session". The dossier labels the 25/38 bracket as second-hand from Chart Fanatics; it is first-hand from March 2026 on.

<!-- target: Section 4 -->
### From the primary corpus: contract sizing, room to fair value and account size

**1. Rules in his own words**

*1.1 Dollars are the invariant; contracts are the dial.* ("The same dollar amount is the most important thing to do on prop firms because it's optimal." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:08:39, L2171); ("now you have a 50 point stop loss and a 76 point take profit. But what you should do is you cut your contract in half." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:08:19, L2161); ("just make like the exact same point stop-loss profit target in points and we would just manipulate your contracts based on what site you're trading with" — JJ, r5AhmGaFujU ~18:58, L594); ("The size of the position should not be subjective. And by position size I mean the contracts and how big your take profit and stop loss are. There must be a statistically optimal size for your take profit, your contracts, and your stop loss in dollar terms." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~6:17, L274). Contracts also follow room to fair value: ("So, you can change the amount of contracts you use based on how many points to the fair price. It is something I do." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~2:57, L104).

*1.2 The half bracket (12.5 / 19) and the minimum room to fair value.* When fewer than about 30 points separate the entry from fair value he halves the bracket and doubles contracts so the dollar outcome is unchanged: ("if there are 30 to 76 points in my favor for the evaluations, then I will normally only have a profit take of 38 points. If there are 15 to 30 points in my favor for the evaluation, then I will do this one for 19 points." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~9:59, L360); ("38 divided by two gives you 19. And then 25 for your stop loss divided by two gives you 12.5. So, you could take a trade of 38 and 25, double the contracts, now it's 19 and 12.5, and it's still the same amount of dollars at risk" — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~9:30, L344); ("remember, I usually do 38 25. But if there aren't 38 points in my favor, in this case, there were only 15, I can do 19 12.5" — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~20:32, L706); ("So maybe I'd go for 19 points, which is half of 38, but double the number of contracts I take because the most important thing is to keep my static profit target the same." — JJ, KhooqEQK9bA ~6:46, L300). The same scaling runs upward: ("would be appropriate for you to do minus 100 points, plus 152 points. That's just one example. Uh, but if it's 170, sometimes you can add, you can use this kind of scaling mechanism that I drew here." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~22:17, L764). Room thresholds for the standard target: ("just for this video, I'm doing the 38 point wins, which means I need 30 points." — JJ, BLvsYJ4sqn8 ~11:19, L381); ("you can't even trade this when it's 14 points away. You could trade this and lose. Uh, where is six? Six is here. We need it to be 30 points away." — JJ, BLvsYJ4sqn8 ~26:56, L862); ("And it's giving 33 and 1/2 points, so target 38 points." — JJ, UVKVSWKFlvo ~8:40, L316). For larger targets the rule is proportional: ("if I want to target 100 point win, then I need at least 80 points in my favor to take that trade. If I only have like 40 points to fair price, there's no way I should let a trade run for 100. 80% 75 if I'm getting a little bit greedy is sort of the mark" — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:20:59, L2548); between two account targets he takes the lower: ("So 50 would probably be a little bit better than a 75 point target in this example, but nonetheless very good for an evaluation giving 3825." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:07:05, L2123).

*1.3 50K versus 150K.* ("In this case, I doubled the stop loss and take profit because I was on a 150k account. What I talk about usually is 25 point stop 38 point take profit. That's good for 50k accounts." — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~2:47, L124); ("So, again same thing 50 point stop, 75-ish point take profit uh on my 150k accounts. Just like very standard for me, and I'll change the amount of contract based on how confident I am in my position." — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~5:46, L221). The reason is the firms' drawdown and payout geometry: ("You have 2.25x the amount of drawdown, and the payout caps are now 2.5x large, like it's going from 2k to 5k." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~13:04, L459); ("Minus 1k plus 2K. It's not going to work on 150K. Now you have way more drawdown. So you might have to even do like minus 2K, plus 4K, 6K, whatever." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~13:20, L468); ("if you have a very good strategy for 30, 40, 50 point trades, and you suddenly move to 150K, that won't generate as much expected value as it would in the 50K trade. So you would need a strategy that takes longer trades." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~8:23, L317). Two 50Ks can stand in for a 150K: ("Basically, you're like replicating a 150K account, but with two 50K accounts. You can also just do half contract size on both of them, and then both 2K's would look like the same thing." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~4:14, L165). On evaluations the money at risk is the fee, so exposure is added on the 50Ks: ("If you're on a 150K eval, it's like 300 bucks. The 50K is 100 bucks. So 350s equal 150 in terms of your actual risk like money at risk." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~17:42, L695) (the caption's "350s" is "three 50s"); ("So start sending 150Ks and then layer in 50Ks. That can also help reduce the risk." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~18:11, L713). The 150K target drops to 38 when fair value is close: ("Uh this one was 38 point take profit. I know I said I usually do 50 and 75. Um that's like I don't know. In general for my 150k accounts, but there's no point in me doing 50 75 here when I believe the fair price is like right here." — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~9:44, L351).

*1.4 Contracts per account.* ("The reason is that in a 50K account you get two contracts and in a 150K account you get a maximum of three contracts" — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~7:43, L294); ("I lost 50, lost 50, then won 250. And adding that up, it should be 150 points for three contracts, which is equivalent to $9,000." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~2:15, L116); ("There's the TP max 10 contract trades. Uh because I got to get in and out pretty quickly." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~21:53, L682); ("I'm trying to use max 15 or 20 MNQ and I enter with the absolute max 15 NQ and the trade just loses like 10 grand in a matter of few minutes on the open" — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~13:00, L399); ("Eight contracts is crazy. Do we teach that in mentoring? I would" — JJ, MIWsdldzJgQ ~14:26, L504; medium). Contract count is derived from the account's dollar target and the points available: ("I have $6,000 profit target and with 50 points that would be six contracts. So that's the reason for that. Just trading a one to one here." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:33:42, L2937); ("The size is solely for the risk management of my account. I need a profit of 7.5k. And it seems to be about 76 points." — JJ, pwO9IAu7FUQ ~10:49, L384). Sizing is standardised, not confidence-weighted, on the psychology side: ("Then you over risk and blow your account. Or "I'm not too confident about this one. I'll just use one contract maybe get a little bit of money." That's That's also not good. You need to have a standardized system." — JJ, 2hwd27aqD30 ~2:18, L113).

*1.5 The ATR ladder is the fxreplay host's.* The 50 / 25 / 16.5 ladder keyed to an ATR above 20, 7-20 and below 7, with 1 / 2 / 3 contracts for about $1,000, is described by the presenter as his own testing: ("if it's over 20, like, price is really moving, um, 50 tick stop loss is going to be a good, um, stop loss distance. And obviously, 1.5 R would be 75." — host, SNO1wqJTq5A ~8:45, L350); ("7 to 20 was kind of the range that I was, uh, testing and playing around with." — host, SNO1wqJTq5A ~8:54, L355); ("it's below 7 ATR, this is most often in the New York p.m. session or probably Asia session sometimes, too, if you're trading that. Um, it's probably better to tighten it up a little further and use like a 16 and 1/2 versus 24.75." — host, SNO1wqJTq5A ~9:06, L361); ("one contract with a 50 tick stop loss equates to $1,000 risk per trade. Two contracts with a 25 tick equates to $1,000 risk per trade, or three contracts with a 16.5 tick stop loss. 1,000." — host, SNO1wqJTq5A ~9:41, L380). What the host attributes to JJ is only the two brackets: ("he says he basically uses like most often a 25 tick stop loss and like a 38.5, um, tick tick profit for 1.5 R." — host, SNO1wqJTq5A ~8:19, L336); ("but he did mention there's like more of maybe if it's more volatile, then he'll do like a 50 tick stop loss and 75 ticks." — host, SNO1wqJTq5A ~8:25, L339). No JJ fact mentions an ATR threshold, a 16.5-point stop or a three-contract tier. The NEXOR host measures the opening-candle body like JJ (("For that, you need to size the body of this opening candle. If that number was bigger than 25 pips, you would have gone with 50 pips of stop loss and 76 pips of target profit." — host, JcW8Wjnw8ck ~1:12, L55)).

- 2026-04-29 (SNO1wqJTq5A, host): the ATR ladder and the $1,000-per-tier arithmetic appear, as the host's rule.

**4. Contradictions**

- Minimum stop: "Never less than 25 points in the New York session" (KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:11:55 L2271, 2026-10-04) against the 12.5-point half bracket (7KJOh2NF-lQ, 74CRg-mID5c, 0uAQUHEB_L8, l6iq0ljhxIo; KhooqEQK9bA names only the 19-point half target). None of the 12.5 examples is tied to the 09:30 session explicitly; unresolved.
- Contract caps: 2 / 3 by account size (4BXpI-hYqe0) against "max 10 contract trades" (PN1UKQMPb5M), "Eight contracts is crazy" (MIWsdldzJgQ, medium) and ("Uh but I sent a huge funded live account technically live account with eight contracts." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~24:18, L802).
- Standardised sizing (2hwd27aqD30 ~2:18 L113) against ("I'll change the amount of contract based on how confident I am in my position." — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~5:46, L221) and ("So, I always risk higher when my chance of winning is expected to be higher." — JJ, 8lbkj0PF1uM ~12:11, L402).
- Against the current dossier and `docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md` #18 ("Published tiers"): the 1 / 2 / 3-contract ladder at about $1,000 is the host's; JJ's own evaluation demonstrations risk $500 per contract on the 25-point stop, with two contracts on 50K accounts. The dossier's wording, *contracts halved when the opening range exceeds 25 points*, is verified first-hand but the measure is the body. `FIRM_PRESETS` give Topstep 50K five contracts and 150K fifteen; JJ says two and three (the quote names no firm), which may be his operating limit rather than the firms' rule.

<!-- target: Section 4 -->
### From the primary corpus: reward-to-risk by account type

**1. Rules in his own words**

*1.1 Evaluations: 1:1.5 because the evaluation itself is 1:1.5.* ("My assessments will have a risk-benefit ratio of 1 to 1.5. The reason is that the valuations have a maximum loss of minus 2K and a target gain of plus 3K. Therefore, it is automatically optimal to make a size of 1 to 1.5 since that is how the evaluation is divided" — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~19:57, L688); ("my standard risk management protocol is a 1:1.5, just because prop firms are minus 2,000 to plus 3,000, meaning it's a 1:1.5 to pass your eval. So, I just separate my trades in a 1:1.5, makes passing very much easier." — JJ, UVKVSWKFlvo ~5:21, L206); ("My personal advice is that you try to trade at least 1 to 1.5 with my strategy if you try mine. The reason is that the funding account assessment has a maximum loss of $2,000 and a profit target of $3,000. That in itself is a ratio of 1 to 1.5." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~5:54, L259). The objective is pass rate: ("The thing is here I'm optimizing for prop firms pass rate because I'm doing a 1 to 1.5. Like there's better strategies if you're trading this on live." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~12:14, L350); ("Eval, just optimize your pass rate. Funded, you optimize your chance of a payout multiplied by size of payout, which is basically like your expected value of that." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~6:52, L254). The constraints are arithmetic: ("Those are math constraints, they are not trading constraints, so make sure you optimize your profit target and stop loss for that specific environment." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~2:51, L160). The ratio is held fixed within an evaluation: ("The risk-benefit ratio that gives you the highest probability of passing your assessment. This is how you decide the X% stop loss and the X% take profit. And then it should be the same for every operation you perform in an evaluation." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~6:51, L296).

*1.2 Consistency-rule funded accounts: 1:1 to 1:1.5, sometimes 1:2, never 1:4.* ("So what I'll do is I'll take a one to 1.5 risk-to-reward on consistency accounts. It's very simple. Same reason as devaluation. Sometimes I'll do a one to two, but this is mostly based on the firm and the profit targets that they assign you to reach for payouts." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~11:19, L418) (the caption's "devaluation" is "the evaluation"); ("with a 1 to four on a consistency account you should never do that." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~44:09, L1422); ("That is another reason why I prioritize the lower risk-to-reward of like a one one or a 1.5 on the consistency accounts. It's because I'm much I'm getting to the goal much faster because the goal here if I'm doing one one is only to hit five R." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~45:04, L1450). Daily cap: ("Some firms will impose a consistency rule on me. So my maximum profit in those firms should be $1,000. Other firms do not have that consistency. I can aim for earnings of three, four, or five thousand dollars without my potential being limited." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~4:24, L200).

*1.3 Funded accounts without a consistency rule: 100-point targets, 1:2 up to 1:6.* ("I'm going to choose to take the 100 point trades on funded accounts that don't have consistency." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~8:38, L336); ("But these entries are better for consistency. And then you can even do one to three, four, five, even six," — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~11:33, L425); ("Just because the risk reward on eval I do 1 to 1.5. Funded sometimes 1 to 2, 1 to 3, 1 to 4, 1 to 5, whatever. That's why I don't have a win rate. I trade so many different risk rewards because it's optimal to do so." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~16:56, L588); ("Obviously I trade a negative or not a negative like a 3R on these accounts. So obviously going to lose a lot more than I win." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~20:15, L633). The account with no payment limit ran 1:5 (the fact's claim field identifies it as E8; the quote names no firm): ("In this case, there was no payment limit. So I was going to use my highest risk-reward strategy, which is why you can see I'm aiming for a 1:5. It's my highest risk-reward ratio because that's what generates the most expected value for me" — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~4:07, L172); ("15,000 was my profit target. 15,000. Definitely a lot. And then, my risk was 3,000 per trade. So, is that one to five?" — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~11:11, L399). FTMO runs 1:4 and suffers in chop: ("I don't really do well on these because they're like a one to4 ristor reward. So they kind of get cooked." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~3:09, L130). The win rate needed falls with the ratio: ("If I'm taking like a one to four risk-to-reward, then my bias only needs to be right about 20% of the time." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~0:45, L95); so does the risk: ("If you have a very low win rate, like you're targeting 1 to six, you'd want to risk less." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~21:23, L726). Funded trades are those with more than 38 points of room: ("normally when they are more than 38, it is a good opportunity to carry out a transaction with a funded account. Simply because 38 or less is usually my evaluation calculation. And then 38 or more, usually 50 or more, to be honest, are funded account trades" — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~2:03, L98). The ratio is a per-site fit and the stop is the free variable: ("if tradeify is a 1 to three, maybe funded next is a 1:2 in the optimal way, but you could run it as a 1:3 because changing your stop loss doesn't affect your EV. So you would just use a different contract size on a different site" — JJ, r5AhmGaFujU ~18:44, L587).

*1.4 The target is what the account needs; the stop follows from the dollar risk.* ("Some prop firms, it's better to have 100 point take profits. Some it's better to have 50. Some it's better to have 25. So I'll optimize in this prop firm specific way by taking funded accounts based on exactly how many points are in my favor." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~7:41, L307); ("I'm look at my dashboard, which is just coded to tell me the optimal risk for all of my accounts, and it's going to say you need 150 points on this one, 200 on this one, 175 on this one. This is closest to 175. So, my profit target is just going to be 175 points." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~58:13, L1852); ("Then the stop loss, if my account is telling me to risk 500, then it's going to be at that amount of points be at 25 points." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~58:44, L1868); ("usually it's like split up by 25 points. Just I guess that's how the the optimization turned out." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:00:46, L1930). The same point values recur across videos: ("And then depending on if your account needs 180 points or if it needs 130 or 100 or 75, whatever." — JJ, EyR3i2GxwyA ~7:01, L261); ("I'm not taking a 78-point take profit. I will be doing 50, 45, 70, whatever my account needs specifically for the optimized value in its specific rule set." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~8:26, L316); ("the risk management on this account indicates that I need 150 points with one contract." — JJ, pwO9IAu7FUQ ~5:06, L197). The stop paired with a 100-point target is 25 on one account and 50 on another: ("I would just enter right here, standard 25 point stop and then take profit even offers 125, but I always go for 100." — JJ, gjWiGBiVis0 ~5:17, L201); ("So, risk 25, go for 100 points most likely here." — JJ, gjWiGBiVis0 ~13:42, L475); ("I sent it for minus 50 plus 100 just because that's what the risk of my account indicated and it lost, whatever." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~29:26, L999); ("A stop loss of 25 points and a take profit of 105. This would probably be good for an account that needs 100 or one that needs 110." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~17:11, L596). Static round numbers beat exact fair-value measurements: ("You could target exactly this price right here, or you could target a static amount of points. Like 76 points, 75, sorry, 75. 38, 37, 50, 100. Like those are very round numbers that work on prop firms because static risk is a little bit better than exact measurements on prop firms," — JJ, gjWiGBiVis0 ~9:13, L329). The daily target is not reduced to chase payout cadence: ("It should be the optimal target. It's going to generate the optimal target is going to generate you more EV than you can strategy-wise." — JJ, r5AhmGaFujU ~18:17, L574).

This tightens the June statement ("Some of them I'll do one to two. Some of them I'll do one to one." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~12:37, L497) to "always" and is the current statement.

**4. Contradictions**

- Evaluations "always" 1:1.5 (74CRg-mID5c ~1:13 L70; KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~9:37 L366, latest) against ("I'll do the one to 1.5 on most of my evaluations. Some of them I'll do one to two. Some of them I'll do one to one." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~12:37, L497).
- 1:1.5 and 38 points called optimal (("1.5 risk-benefit ratio simply because it is the most optimal for evaluations." — JJ, 0uAQUHEB_L8 ~15:49, L596); ("my target is exactly 38 points, as that's the statistically optimal profit target based on my contract size and current account." — JJ, KN7j6NXXAio ~7:50, L320)) against ("I'll use 750 and 500 as my risk management for every single trade. Now, obviously that's not optimal." — JJ, BLvsYJ4sqn8 ~2:55, L122) and ("The precise risk management is what I review in my mentoring, simply because I don't want it to be public." — JJ, KN7j6NXXAio ~27:32, L1029). The public bracket is a stand-in for per-account values he withholds.
- Static versus many ratios: ("You should never change your risk-reward balance. It should be static." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~13:24, L552) against ("That's why I don't trade a strategy that has a static or set risk to reward for every single trade." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~7:10, L336); reconciled by ("And then funded accounts, some I'll do one to one. Some I'll do one to 1.5, one to four, whatever. So, I have a a ton of different risk rewards. I cycle through all of them. And then if I see a one to 1.5 in the market, I know I'm going to choose this account" — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~12:46, L502): fixed per account, varied across accounts.
- Against the current dossier: Section 4's wording, *1:1.5 on evaluations and consistency-rule accounts, 100-point targets on funded accounts without a consistency rule (unverified)*, is verified in his words and widened (consistency accounts also 1:1 and sometimes 1:2; non-consistency 1:3 to 1:6). Section 10 lists *the stop he pairs with the reported 100-point funded target* as unknown; he shows 25 (gjWiGBiVis0) and 50 (7KJOh2NF-lQ), by account.

<!-- target: Section 4 -->
### From the primary corpus: dollar risk per trade and survival

**1. Rules in his own words**

*1.1 Dollar risk on an evaluation.* ("You can risk about 500 to $2,000 per trade, profit target about 1,000 to 1,500 per trade." — JJ, wsKx7L9EJys ~1:02, L55), bounded on both sides: ("You don't want to be risking too small because the trailing drawdown will eat you up, and you don't want to be risking too much because then you're not going to pass it within the consistency rule, and you're going to risk much higher, increasing your chance of blowing the account." — JJ, wsKx7L9EJys ~1:08, L58). The demonstrated unit is $500 / $750 per NQ contract: ("I'm just going to risk 500, go for 750 every single trade." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~0:59, L52); ("Just buy. I'll use 38. I'll use 750 and 500 as my risk management for every single trade. Now, obviously that's not optimal." — JJ, BLvsYJ4sqn8 ~2:55, L122); ("when you're risking 500, it does take a while to pass the account." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~14:14, L522; medium). Target ceiling: ("you can go for 500, you can go for 750, you can go for 1,000, you can go for 1,500, but don't go for more than 1500 on eBunded." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~45:44, L1238; medium; the caption's "eBunded" is "eval. Funded"). The final evaluation trade is sized differently: ("the third one that's when the account is worth the most is when it's one day from the funded. That becomes a little bit better to risk half of the account, essentially giving yourself two attempts." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~4:38, L201); ("So, I would try implementing the 1R instead of the negative or the sub one risk reward just for the final trade. It should help the pass rate a little bit on the end." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~5:37, L238), because ("And with these 40% accounts, it usually ends up having like 4,500 of drawdown and then only an $1,800 profit target. So that asymmetric risk where it's basically sub one RR is not really something that you want to have maximum exposure to." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~4:46, L206). The money truly at risk on an evaluation is the fee: ("if you were to risk the entire account, just all 4,500, well, you already know, okay, wow, I'm risking the whole account. That's $250. If you risk half, okay, that's $125." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~17:35, L529). On Lucid the unit was 1,200 / 1,800, a 1:1.5: ("So, minus one plus 1.5. That was the risk management approach that got me on Lucid this insane return." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~7:25, L271); ("one account at a time greatly improves your psychology because you're only losing 1,200 and then the next trade you make 1,800." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~9:31, L341). His own ceiling: ("I feel like the most I've ever risked in a trade is probably $2,000 ever. And I make $100,000 a month trading a prop firm." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~12:44, L448). Fixed percent risk inside an evaluation: ("And in evaluation environments, using a fixed X% risk for your account is much more optimal than using a non-fixed one based on what the market shows you." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~6:36, L286).

*1.2 Dollar boxes on funded accounts.* ("I do take static risk and static profit targets. Like each of my accounts, I know exactly on this account I'm going to risk 1,000, go for 2,000. This account I'm going to risk 1,000, go for 1,500. And then maybe here risk 500, go for 2,000." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~14:59, L577); ("The reward is a static number. It will be exactly a profit of one thousand dollars, exactly two thousand, three thousand, five hundred." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~7:42, L284); ("So, this was my funded account. 3K was my profit. I made roughly $3,000 in this account because I had a daily loss limit of $ 3,000." — JJ, MIWsdldzJgQ ~6:09, L233); ("So, huge trade for 114 points in your favor. I sent two fundeds. this one for 7K and then this one for 10,000. I believe it was four contracts on this one and then three contracts on this one." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~16:20, L549). The $1,800 daily target the dossier attributes to his accounts is a student's: ("All of his earnings are $1,800. Or pretty close to it. It should be $1,800." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~8:55, L324); the two student accounts ran 1:1.5 and 1:1: ("Just make it less than 1,200 because it's a 1: 1.5 and that's what my strategy works best for me personally." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~10:20, L371; medium); ("Same thing here, but I told him to do a 1:1. So, if he was going to look for 1,200, he needs to risk 1,200." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~10:26, L374). Very large single-trade targets are rejected: ("Another strategy that doesn't usually work in funding firms is setting extremely high profit targets. People looking for $10,000 in a single transaction, or even more than" — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~8:09, L345). Why a target is not pushed higher: ("Now, why don't you just go for an infinitely large win? Because your probability of losing goes up a lot. So, whatever you gain by trying to win more, in this case, going for an extra 200 bucks, you'd also lose an extra 200 bucks, right? Because your risk is now a,000 instead of 800 on that trade." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~16:26, L480).

*1.3 The values change with the account's state.* ("if I'm on an eval with Topstep, that's a different RR, different profit target, different stop loss than a funded with Topstep, which is different than a funded account that is new, different than a funded account that is in loss, different than a funded account that is in profit, different than a funded account that's taking a payout." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~8:35, L321); ("I'm changing my profit targets based on the expected value of my account." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~14:45, L533); ("in evaluations I only do 1.5 RR. In anchored areas, you can change it a little. If you're in a newly funded account versus one with profits, obviously your expected value changes by about 5 times." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~27:34, L942; medium; the caption's "anchored" is "funded"). The generator is a simulation he does not publish: ("So, it simulates millions of combinations of take-profit and stop losses per firm, per account type, per balance, per draw down. Then, it outputs the one with a statistically optimal take-profit and stop-loss." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~3:06, L134); ("if I was to show you, I obviously cannot show you 1,800 different takeprofits, 1,800 different stop- losses, and list out every single firm for you." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~11:17, L419); ("So most important thing that you should do is size for the state of the account and then rerun the numbers when the state changes. That's what I have my dashboard do." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~10:24, L388). Account choice precedes risk: ("Next, choose your account before the risk. Obviously, do not just say, "I'm going to risk $500 a trade." Don't do that. Uh, it needs to be correct for that firm, for that account or your account stage, and your payout conditions." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~34:15, L1002). Inputs to the decision: ("which prop firm we're on, what our balance is, what our drawdown is, if we're eval or funded, if we're going to a live account, what is the optimal way to approach that to generate the most expected value given our rule set." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~13:50, L499).

*1.4 Survival constraints.* ("Since the reduction is dynamic, you need to make sure you can survive more than four consecutive losses to avoid hitting the maximum loss in a bad run, especially as your funded account balance grows and becomes worth significantly more." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~4:46, L214); ("Lastly, bankroll management. That is how you win at poker in the long run. Same thing with prop firms. Okay. I would prefer your risk of ruin to be less than 0.5%." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~21:54, L630); ("Imagine a maximum loss of $ 2,000 in our account and we are going to risk $500 per trade. Obviously, if you lose four in a row, you 'll have lost your account." — JJ, PMGhHCjYG5c ~10:42, L463); ("You need to optimize for your survival. You should adjust the size according to the drawdown assigned to you, not according to your actual positions" — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~11:02, L456); ("First, adjust the size so that a losing streak doesn't drain your only account if you only have one. Second, if you have five accounts, adjust it so that a losing streak doesn't wipe out all five of your accounts at once." — JJ, 4IGbxmKJ4BU ~5:18, L255); ("The way I think about it, I'm not risking $3,000 per trade, I'm just risking one unit per trade. A predefined calculated risk is based on my account [music] and my edge." — JJ, RGYbTD1j628 ~3:41, L168; medium); ("just search up Kelly Criterion. It's pretty easy to understand and you can learn specifically like bankroll optimized sizing." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~3:08, L115). Edge, frequency and fees set size: ("these three things are pretty much all that will determine your position size. Nothing else will determine your position size. Neither the inputs, nor the indicators, nor the configuration." — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~8:31, L343), and higher risk shortens days per attempt at the cost of EV per attempt: ("if you risk less, you have a greater opportunity to take advantage of your edge, which is why the expected value is higher with lower risk. However, a lower risk will make it take longer to approve the funded account" — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~7:21, L300). Sizing errors dominate: ("I've lost far more accounts due to poor sizing than due to bad operations." — JJ, PMGhHCjYG5c ~14:02, L598).

<!-- target: Section 4 -->
### From the primary corpus: the evaluation objective, the evaluation posture and the 1:1.5 derivation

Source: `docs/research/extracted/_by_group/eval_risk_math.json`, 482 machine-verified facts from 52 of the 58 transcripts (468 spoken by JJ, 6 by hosts, 8 by students; 452 high confidence, 29 medium, 1 low). Upload dates are from `transcript-index.md`. Captions are quoted as they are; mis-hearings are explained in prose. Where a quote comes from a sibling group (risk_sizing, stops_psychology, firms_payouts, accounts_execution) the group is named.

#### 1. The evaluation objective in his words

The 50k example he uses everywhere is the rule set, not the label: ("They have an account label of $50,000, but once you realize you're only given $2,000 of max loss, you have a $3,000 profit target, you have a 50% consistency rule." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~2:04, L128). ("You have to make $3,000 before losing $2,000 on most of the common 50K evaluations and then follow a 50% consistency rule or sometimes even 40%." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~2:45, L156). The objective is a single probability: ("The only goal of the eval is to reach your profit target before hitting the max loss and then follow the consistency rule if there is one." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~2:39, L152); ("when you plug into a prop firm, you want to optimize for your pass rate, which is your chance of reaching your profit target before your max loss." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~0:25, L44); ("I don't care what your win rate is, I don't care your risk to reward. All I care is how many times you hit plus 3K before minus 2K. That is your entire goal in this simulated account." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~6:57, L270). He frames it as a first-passage problem: ("there are boundary problems and you optimize for your chance of of hitting one boundary before the other." — JJ, 1CEAgRUNZ4c ~16:31, L619); ("I basically solved for the highest chance of passing the eval." — JJ, 1CEAgRUNZ4c ~16:55, L633). Fees and time enter only as secondary terms: ("You can account for time. You probably don't need to account for fees." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~4:01, L166), and once an evaluation is in profit the objective switches: ("once you start making money, you want to optimize for your time instead of your profit or your pass rate." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~4:13, L173). Passing says nothing about the next stage: ("passing the eval doesn't mean that you're going to get a payout. It does not even increase your chances of getting a payout based on if you're doing good on an eval" — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~2:30, L146).

#### 2. Evaluation posture: capped downside, maximum aggression, few trades

("Rule two, once you have your model, attack the evaluations aggressively. An eval is not a funded account. The most important thing to understand is that your risk is completely capped. You basically have no downside." — JJ, RGYbTD1j628 ~1:15, L72). ("Your only job on an eval is to pass it as efficiently and as quickly as possible. Be aggressive within your rules, hit the profit target, and get funded." — JJ, RGYbTD1j628 ~1:51, L96). ("I up my risk on the evals just so I'm able to pass them quickly and then get to the funded account stage." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~1:44, L82). The reason is the calculator's months-to-payout term: ("I'm really aggressive on the evals just to pass them super quick. That way I can decrease the months to payout, which obviously gives me more money per month." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~12:13, L452); ("I wouldn't try to take two weeks to pass an evaluation. I want to finish it within a week. So I take pretty aggressive trades." — JJ, g68HHbrIBBg ~14:30, L551). Passing in two to four days leaves the month for the winning-day requirement: ("two, three, or four days for the evaluation. Perhaps 15 would increase to 18, 19, or 20." — JJ, 0uAQUHEB_L8 ~14:17, L541).

How many trades: ("high risk and high reward in assessments to finish soon. Now, from the assessments, you know that it will probably take you two to four operations to pass it." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~27:09, L926); in the same video ("Two operations are needed to pass the evaluation, five winning days to receive payment. So, that's a minimum of seven trades to earn 1k." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~18:04, L626). The only dollar sizes he gives for a 50k evaluation are a range: ("Risk 500 to 1,000 per trade, profit target 1,000 to 1,500 per trade." — JJ, wsKx7L9EJys ~1:27, L68). Other evaluation sizes in the corpus are smaller fractions of the drawdown: on FTMO evaluations ("I didn't lose the evals technically. Like I lost a third of them, only risking a third of it." — JJ, HlWSP7ajgpQ ~4:06, L160), priced as ("if you're losing one/ird of the eval and an eval is 270 bucks you only lose $90 of expected value." — JJ, HlWSP7ajgpQ ~4:56, L186; "one/ird" is "one third"); in Miami ("using a contract. Double the stop loss. And this is just to pass an evaluation and obtain funding." — JJ, MIWsdldzJgQ ~14:03, L491, medium confidence); and his own simulation runs the evaluation ten times over, which is ten evaluations rather than ten trades: ("it's going to split into 10 attempts. Eval plus 3K before minus 2K and then we'll monitor the end of day max loss as it trails." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~13:02, L480). On a last-day pass he suggests one or two trades: ("I would consider doing something like plus or minus 1,800 first and then from there taking a second trade if you ended up losing and you're trying to pass it that same day" — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~4:55, L212, risk_sizing); ("Don't layer the third day as well." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~12:04, L482).

Evaluation targets in points: ("3825 in all its evaluations. It's the best for assessments." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~2:39, L117; 38-point target, 25-point stop); ("if you're trading evals, you usually don't want 100 points cuz they have the consistency rule." — JJ, gjWiGBiVis0 ~11:05, L390); ("50 points on funded is pretty good for continuation and 38 on eval." — JJ, gjWiGBiVis0 ~11:50, L414). He accepts losing streaks on evaluations: ("five losses, four wins. But I don't really care. I'm trying to complete my assessments fairly quickly." — JJ, MIWsdldzJgQ ~6:57, L259; "assessments" is the caption for evaluations).

A cheap eval is the only thing at risk: ("when you're in an evaluation, the only thing at risk is your evaluation fee, whether it's one hundred, two hundred, or three hundred dollars." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~2:18, L119); ("if you're going to get frustrated, at least bet only on evaluations. They're probably worth a tenth of your funded account" — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~12:33, L519). The tilt rule follows: ("you buy 10 evaluations for a hundred dollars each, you could full port 10 evaluations straight, get more fun out of it, and it's the exact same risk as doing that one-to-one plus or minus thousand dollar trade." — JJ, 2hwd27aqD30 ~6:34, L268), and even then ("If you're full porting, probably get two or three." — JJ, 2hwd27aqD30 ~7:11, L290).

#### 3. The 1:1.5 derivation and the trailing-drawdown mechanism

First stated 2026-03-28: ("most evaluations are plus 3,000 before they're minus 2,000. And essentially, that's three to two, which is 1.5 risk to reward." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~12:32, L446). Restated 2026-06-22 (aCOgfvL6lK8 ~7:48, L335), 2026-07-11: ("This, in itself, is a risk-benefit ratio of 1 to 1.5. Therefore, if you split your trades in a 1 to 1.5 ratio" — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~7:36, L325), 2026-07-25: ("split your trades in a 1:1.5 ratio and you'll automatically have a higher approval rate just from how the loss limit behaves." — JJ, KN7j6NXXAio ~5:50, L248), 2026-08-06: ("you'll automatically have a higher pass rate simply because of how that dynamic drawdown adjusts after each trade you make." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~1:49, L100), and latest, 2026-10-04: ("the evaluation has a $3,000 profit target and then a $2,000 max loss. This in and of itself is a 1 to 1.5. So if you separate your trades into a 1 to 1.5, you're automatically going to have a higher pass rate just because of how the draw down trails." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~8:22, L328); ("finding a good risk-to-reward for the evaluation which is is probably just going to be 1 to 1.5 for everybody if it's a minus 2k plus 3k" — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~21:53, L741).

The mechanism is that a trailing drawdown never adds room: ("then you still only have $2,000 worth of draw down. At least you're $1,000 in profit, but you didn't make any new draw down to trade with." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~21:47, L738). Hence target size changes the pass rate at constant edge: ("going for a $1,000 of profit, or 1,500 profit, or 300 profit, are all going to have different pass rates because they're fitting into the rules differently as the drawdown trails." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~1:55, L95); ("You can optimize your take profit and stop loss because they're all going to generate the same EV in a biased environment." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~4:59, L199); ("the best approach on a prop firm is to find the one that has the highest pass rate specifically, and then execute that as many times as possible." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~2:07, L102). Small targets fail by arithmetic: ("you win 200, you lose 200. How many days do you think it's going to take you to reach 3K? Right? Even with the draw down trailing, even if the draw down doesn't trail, you still need to win 15 times more than you lose." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~15:26, L468); for a 1:10 style ("it's going to take them forever to hit that plus 3,000 or they're going to need three in a row, which with 1:10 is like extremely unlikely." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~9:41, L398); ("by reducing it from one thousand to perhaps one hundred, your success rate will worsen by exactly 33%." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~8:05, L342, medium confidence, surrounding captions garbled). On the point target he fixes exactly 1.5R: ("19 is the one that, at 1.5, generates the most expected value for me in the evaluations and probably for you too, which is why I'm aiming for exactly 1.5 instead of dragging this down and letting it be 1.8, because that's not optimal for a funding firm." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~26:43, L914).

Break-even at 1.5R: ("the break-even point in that would be 40%. But anything above 40% and you're going to make money in funding companies, for sure." — JJ, KN7j6NXXAio ~16:22, L627); ("A strategy with no advantage would be a 40% success rate with a risk-reward ratio of 1.5, which gives zero advantage per trade." — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~0:16, L40). His own stated edge over it is small: ("The break-even rate on 1.5 R operations is 40%. Therefore, I am 2% above the break-even point" — JJ, 4IGbxmKJ4BU ~0:56, L68); ("50% with 1.5 R is statistically impossible with a sufficiently large sample size" — JJ, 4IGbxmKJ4BU ~1:13, L80). Against that, a session sample: ("Four wins, four losses. That's 50% at 1.5. That's 10% above the break-even point, which is literally printing money out of thin air." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~32:50, L1116). Given rules, one combination is optimal: ("given the set of rules, you must have an optimal risk-reward ratio." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~4:59, L223); ("Your success rate and your risk-reward ratio are inversely related. So you need to find out which one works on the funding account and test it." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~5:45, L253).

#### 5. Evolution over time

- 2026-04-18 (RGYbTD1j628): attack evaluations aggressively; downside capped at the fee.

- 2026-06-22 to 2026-08-06 (aCOgfvL6lK8, J8qXxtFFpGg, KN7j6NXXAio, S8fSNbPFuLA): 1:1.5 restated four times with the trailing-drawdown reason; "two to four operations" (AxP-cg50TdM, 2026-07-30).
- 2026-08-16 (0uAQUHEB_L8): the one dissent, 1.5 "not optimal for funding assessments" (L76), alongside a 1:2 break-even of 33% (L296).
- 2026-08-23 and 2026-09-08 (74CRg-mID5c, DEkzU8gqX-o): exactly 1.5R at 19 points; TP/SL tuned for pass rate because they carry the same EV in a biased environment.
- 2026-09-13 and 2026-09-15 (HlWSP7ajgpQ, sWJa8vRfPb8): one third of the drawdown per trade on FTMO; time, not pass rate, once in profit; the evaluation simulated as ten attempts.
- 2026-10-04 (KHEQ5g55dQ4): 1:1.5 "for everybody" on -2k/+3k, the current statement.

#### 6. Contradictions

1. 1.5R "not optimal for funding assessments" (0uAQUHEB_L8, 2026-08-16) versus 1:1.5 optimal for evaluations in every other video including the latest; the 1:2 break-even of 33% he computes in the same video (L296) is the comparison he had in mind.
2. Trades per evaluation: exactly two (dossier, jjwebinar.com) versus "two to four" (AxP-cg50TdM L926), one third of the drawdown per trade on FTMO (HlWSP7ajgpQ L160) and "Double the stop loss" with one contract (MIWsdldzJgQ L491, medium confidence). Dossier item 12, which says his actual evaluation risk figure is not published, is too strong: the published figures are a range.
3. Win rate: 42% at 1.5R as his own figure (4IGbxmKJ4BU L68) versus "Four wins, four losses. That's 50% at 1.5" in a session recap (74CRg-mID5c L1116), the 57.5% sample in the dossier and the 54% third-party figure used by the code.
4. "I don't care what your win rate is, I don't care your risk to reward" (XhsbfEdJBAc L270) reads against the 1:1.5 rule; in context the first is the objective (pass rate) and the second the instrument chosen to maximise it.
5. The dossier quotes the 1:1.5 rule from a sozai transcription; the verbatim captions are at KHEQ5g55dQ4 L328 and 3L8xdh3oPm4 L446.
