### From the primary corpus: the funded objective, risk coming down, the optimal daily target and the Chart Fanatics "optimal risk" reasoning

Source as in the previous block; sibling groups are named where used.

#### 1. Funded objective and posture

("on your funded account, you have a completely different set of constraints, new set of math, new optimization problem to solve for because you no longer care about your pass rate" — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~3:00, L166); ("if I'm given a funded account, what is my average payout amount over the lifetime of that account?" — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~3:09, L172). The objective is cash out, not P&L or survival: ("When you get to the funded account, you want to optimize for your expected cash withdrawal. Nothing else matters. It doesn't matter how much money you make. It doesn't matter how long your account survives. The only thing that matters is how much in payouts can you get from one account." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~4:20, L177). Yet survival is the lever: ("So your goal in the funding firm, instead of maximizing profits, should be to reduce your probability of reaching maximum loss." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~0:51, L61); ("if you stop reaching the maximum loss, your account will eventually experience exponential growth." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~0:57, L65). ("Zero is permanent. As soon as you lose your account, there is obviously no more profit potential in it." — JJ, 4IGbxmKJ4BU ~3:33, L180).

Risk comes down as the account's value rises: ("I hope you understand why I would reduce my risk as my funded accounts increase their balance." — JJ, 0uAQUHEB_L8 ~11:54, L456) because "if the expected value is equal, your goal should be to reduce variance as much as possible." (same fact). The size is set by what the account is worth, which he prices by payout proximity: ("If you're up 2K, then we'll just assume that your value is $1,000 because you could take $1,000 payout from the account. If your account is fresh, let's assume it's worth $410 because that's how much it cost you to get to the funded account." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~9:03, L341); the same $1,000 stop ("is actually losing $500 of value, whereas this one is only losing $200 of value." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~9:42, L364, medium); ("And you could technically optimize around the same thing as having a $2,000 live account." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~10:46, L401). Real-risk arithmetic on a fresh account: ("if you're risking $1,000 of the simulated money, your real risk is $150 because that's half of what you paid to get there, and you're risking half of what you have." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~8:59, L325); once up $2,000, ("my actual risk is $1,000 because in the long run, my expected value of this is $1,000 worth of payouts that I could be getting." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~9:17, L335). ("chips are not equal to dollars, that is, simulated money does not equal real dollars, taking what appears to be, or statistically could be, a positive EV trade may not actually be positive EV, because you are increasing your risk where you shouldn't" — JJ, PMGhHCjYG5c ~12:16, L527).

Concrete funded sizes: on a fresh funded account at one firm ("make less than $1,200 plus $ 1,800 in this specific account, this specific site, and this specific plan in the funded account." — JJ, xNeBEqv06k0 ~6:26, L264, medium; "less than" is the caption for "minus"), then ("After the first payment, it changes to minus 1200, plus 1200 risk management, or plus 1000 risk management." — JJ, xNeBEqv06k0 ~6:42, L274), ("so that when you go live, you don't lose too much profit from your account." — JJ, xNeBEqv06k0 ~6:56, L283). Static dollar risk and target per account: ("on this account I'm going to risk 1,000, go for 2,000. This account I'm going to risk 1,000, go for 1,500. And then maybe here risk 500, go for 2,000." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~14:59, L577, risk_sizing). Sized to the daily loss limit: ("I made roughly $3,000 in this account because I had a daily loss limit of $ 3,000." — JJ, MIWsdldzJgQ ~6:09, L233, risk_sizing). Ceiling: ("the most I've ever risked in a trade is probably $2,000 ever." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~12:44, L448, risk_sizing). Funded targets are larger and nearer fair price: ("Normally, with funded accounts, I try to aim as close to the fair price as possible." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~3:07, L133; 38 points on an evaluation, 56 on a funded account in that example); ("my funded accounts are usually going for more than 30 points just cuz I like to risk risk heavy." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~14:04, L477); and entries are stricter: ("On evals, I will take displacement candle entries, but funded accounts, I like to wait for break of structure just because it's a stronger entry criteria." — JJ, xTxrtFKlQfc ~4:57, L194). He sometimes runs a different strategy altogether: ("personally I trade different strategies on Eval and Funded just because they're like incredibly different." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~6:01, L275).

Sizing from losing streaks: ("If you risk $500 on 100 trades, it is virtually guaranteed statistically that you will burn through at least one account with a risk of $500 per trade." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~8:02, L342); ("if you only have $2,000 of loss margin and risk about $500 per trade, in 100 trades, there is a 91% chance that you will blow your account." — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~1:56, L105); ("the number of times you can make a mistake in a row gives you a good margin of trades before reaching your loss limit. And that should help you define the size of your positions." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~7:00, L302).

#### 2. The optimal daily profit target

("Every single account gets a daily profit target and a hard daily loss limit." — JJ, RGYbTD1j628 ~2:34, L124, stops_psychology); ("Before I even open a chart, I already know my two numbers for the day, my strict profit target and my strict daily loss limit for every single account I have." — JJ, RGYbTD1j628 ~2:55, L138, stops_psychology). The target is a static dollar figure: ("The reward is a static number. It will be exactly a profit of one thousand dollars, exactly two thousand, three thousand, five hundred." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~7:42, L284, risk_sizing). The $1,800 figure in the dossier is a student's new funded account: ("All of his earnings are $1,800. Or pretty close to it. It should be $1,800." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~8:55, L324, risk_sizing), which changes after the first payout: ("Now, the optimal profit target is any value between 1,000 and 1,200, which should have been followed here." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~9:26, L341, firms_payouts); on an account with unlimited payouts ("$15,000 was the optimal profit target for this account. The reason? They had unlimited payments." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~12:36, L446, firms_payouts). His own targets move with account value: ("it was 24, 24, 24. Now, my count is worth a different amount, 46, 46, 46." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~14:38, L529, risk_sizing, medium). Trade count is free: ("We could take five trades to get to the optimal target. We could take one, we could take two, we could take three. That doesn't really matter. The only thing that matters is hitting it by the end of the day." — JJ, r5AhmGaFujU ~18:25, L578, risk_sizing); ("I like my accounts to either be at profit target or gone." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:39:08, L3103, stops_psychology). The general claim: ("there must exist a statistically optimal profit target and a statistically optimal stop loss to exist within those environments at any given win rate or risk to reward." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~1:43, L88, risk_sizing); the per-state table is his product: ("there are 1,800 different combinations at the minimum, right? Based on the value of an account and how that changes." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~16:05, L586, risk_sizing); ("based on my dashboard which tells me the optimal risk for that specific account, because you have an optimal risk and an optimal profit target." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~2:06, L99, accounts_execution).

#### 3. The "optimal risk" reasoning in the Chart Fanatics episode (KHEQ5g55dQ4, 2026-10-04)

Verbatim, the episode's risk argument is: the 1:1.5 split from the -2k/+3k shape (L328, L741); the trailing drawdown adds nothing (L738); cost to funded ("an evaluation like $100 divided by your pass rate. If your pass rate is 25%, that means you pass one out of four. So, it cost you $400 to achieve your funded account." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~23:49, L800); a blown funded account costs only that: ("Hitting your max loss, all you lose is $400. you don't lose the $2,000 of SIM capital or whatever. So, you only lose 400." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~24:40, L826); his realised rate ("kind of only lost like three or 400 bucks here because the eval um got like 33% pass rate." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:25:51, L2697); and the risk-of-ruin passage (position-based ~1:17:01-1:17:50 in the extraction, ~1:14:48 in the GB10 notes): "you need to calculate your risk of ruin and you want it to be below 5%." (L2427); "take your pass rate, maybe it's like 33% or 30%. Take your payout rate, meaning your chance of getting a payout on the funded account. Multiply these two together" (L2430); "if I buy an evout, I have a 0.09% 09% chance of getting a payout, which means if I buy an Eval, I have a 91% chance of getting nothing." (L2436, medium); "nine times out of 10, you lose $100. So you lose 900, but that time you win and you do get that payout, it's likely going to be about $2,000 or more," (L2442); "do 0.91 to the power of 10. If you have $1,000 in your bank account, that will get you 10 evaluations. So that would be your chance of losing everything." (L2452). The garble resolves arithmetically: 0.30 x 0.30 = 0.09, spoken as zero point zero nine, nine percent and captioned 0.09% 09%; the complement is 91%, and 0.91^10 = 0.389, so the bankroll of ten $100 evaluations has a 39% chance of zero payouts. No "optimal risk" number beyond these appears in the episode; the per-account sizing is the dashboard he does not publish.

#### 5. Evolution over time

- 2026-05-10 (wsKx7L9EJys): funded sizing identical to the evaluation; at most a 10% performance drop (L113).
- 2026-05-28 to 2026-06-25 (vJuwbKibN1E, C-_0QhKZmA4, XhsbfEdJBAc, aCOgfvL6lK8, 2hwd27aqD30): real risk equals acquisition cost; ("I do risk different on every site." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~6:56, L255); funded traded slower than evaluations; different strategies on the two; tilt routed to evaluations, full port only on a fresh funded account.
- 2026-07-11 to 2026-07-30 (J8qXxtFFpGg, xNeBEqv06k0, AxP-cg50TdM): minimise probability of max loss; risk-reward drops to 1:1 after the first payout; static dollar targets per account state ($1,800, then $1,000-1,200).
- 2026-08-16 to 2026-08-23 (0uAQUHEB_L8, PMGhHCjYG5c, 74CRg-mID5c): risk reduced as funded balances grow; chips are not dollars; the account is worth $0 once breached; funded targets aim at fair price.
- 2026-09-03 to 2026-09-15 (2BtwoTCxcbc, sWJa8vRfPb8): per-stage sizing (evaluation, funded, funded near payout, funded after payout, live); account-state values ($410 fresh, $1,000 when $2k up); expected cash withdrawal as the sole funded objective.
- 2026-10-04 (KHEQ5g55dQ4): the episode's argument is the 1:1.5 split, cost to funded, the capped loss and the risk-of-ruin chain; no funded sizing number is given.

#### 6. Contradictions

1. Funded sizing equals evaluation sizing (wsKx7L9EJys, May 2026; "make sure your performance doesn't change when you move from the evaluation to the funded account" — JJ, KhooqEQK9bA ~0:35, L53) versus ("that obvious way will give you a 5.1% probability and you'll earn about $11 a year. Basically, you break even" — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~4:31, L196) and ("you're giving yourself the same ruleset across both. Not optimal" — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~3:56, L166). The September 2026 statements are later; KhooqEQK9bA concerns the edge, not the size.
2. Fresh funded account valued at its cost ($300-$1,000) versus ("my expected value is going to generate me exactly a $1,000 payout on average" — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~6:29, L214) and ("realistically you can get $2,250 of expected value" — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~5:07, L199) on $4,500 of drawdown. He uses cost for risk accounting and expected payout for return; the two meet only through his 3x target.
3. The dossier line that the optimal profit target on his accounts was $1,800 per day describes a student's account and one of his own first-stage plans at one firm; after the first payout it is $1,000-1,200, and on other accounts 24 and 46 (units not captioned, 4BXpI-hYqe0 L529, medium) or 15,000.
4. The dossier's posture sentence (aggressive to get funded, conservative to stay funded) and the jjwebinar.com phrase about risk coming right down are summary wording; the verbatim basis is XhsbfEdJBAc L452 and L457 and 0uAQUHEB_L8 L456.
5. "The only thing that matters is how much in payouts can you get from one account." (sWJa8vRfPb8 L177) versus "It doesn't matter how long your account survives." in the same sentence and "reduce your probability of reaching maximum loss" (J8qXxtFFpGg L61): survival is the means, not the objective; he sizes to the daily loss limit (MIWsdldzJgQ L233) while targeting a static dollar figure.

### From the primary corpus: cost to funded, cost per dollar of drawdown, the expected value of an evaluation and the eval-fee inequality

Source as in the first block.

#### 1. Definitions in his words

Expected value: ("the formula for expected value is your chance of a win times how much you win minus your chance of a loss times how much you lose in any given scenario." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~5:42, L204); ("if you would have a 50% chance of making $1,000, that straight up is just $500 in expected value. You just multiply those two together." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~0:26, L41). Pass rate: ("if you buy 10 accounts and pass three, your rate is three out of 10. It's simple math, that would be a 30% approval rate." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~1:15, L71). Funded-account expected value: ("The opportunity, or the probability of payment, multiplied by the size of the payment, will give you a fairly accurate and good estimate of your expected value." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~3:13, L136); measured from a sample: ("if you have 50 funded accounts and you get 50k in payouts, you know my expected value per funded account is $1,000." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~29:22, L963). Risk of ruin: ("risk of ruin is talking about your chance of losing everything. And you want that chance to be as low as low as possible, of course, but you can never make it zero." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~1:15, L61). Variance: ("what are the chances that you're able to pass an evaluation and get a payout with that set amount of money. And that's based on your pass rate and your chance of getting a payout obviously." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~20:17, L672). Expectancy over win rate: ("Expected value is more important than the success rate" — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~4:42, L212); ("What matters is your average profit per trade." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~5:25, L240).

#### 2. Cost to funded

("Take the cost of an eval and divide it by your pass rate to see your cost of funded." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~6:42, L317). Worked: ("If you pass three out of 10, you just divide it by 0.3. That will give you the cost of being funded, which would be about 333." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~1:31, L80); ("let's say you have a 33% pass rate, and the eval costs $100. This means you're passing one out of three, and your average cost to achieve the funded account is $300." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~3:38, L146); ("If your pass rate is 25%, that means you pass one out of four. So, it cost you $400 to achieve your funded account." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~23:49, L800); 150k: ("we're going to pay 250 for an assessment, and we're going to pass one out of every three. That means our average funding cost, using this formula here, is $750. This will give us $4,500 of drawdown margin. This is in the 150K account." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~4:29, L178). The website version adds activation: ("eval cost divided by pass rate plus activation fee if there is one." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~16:25, L574), and cost should include resets: ("your cost should also technically include the activation fee, every eval that you purchase, every reset that you do" — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~7:33, L236). An evaluation is worth its price: ("If you pay 100 bucks, it's worth about $100." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~3:26, L139); the fresh funded account is worth what it cost: ("from this $2,000, your real expected value of this account is $300, because you paid $300 for it." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~3:56, L156).

#### 3. Cost per dollar of drawdown

("if I pay $750 and get $4,500 of loss margin, then my cost per dollar of loss margin is 16.6 cents. So, if I give a funding company 16.6 cents, they will give me $1 of loss margin." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~4:44, L186). The $750 is the cost to funded ($250 / one-in-three), not the fee; the dossier and `risk.cost_per_drawdown_dollar` pass the fee. The September version states the reciprocal: ("take the drawdown of the funded account and divide it by the cost per funded account to see what is your cost per drawdown on a funded account." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~6:47, L320). Expected value is bounded by the drawdown: ("When you're given $2,000 of loss margin, if it costs you $1,000 to get past it, realistically you can't get more than half of that in expected value. So, in the account with $4,500 of loss margin, realistically you can get $2,250 of expected value." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~5:07, L199).

#### 4. Expected value of an evaluation and the eval-fee inequality

Latest formulation (2026-09-29): ("Multiply the probability of the upside by how much the upside is and then you subtract your fee." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~6:34, L209); ("The reason why we're not subtracting a probability times a fee is because we already paid that fee. So, it's basically a 100% chance that we had to pay the eval fee." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~6:47, L215); ("we are receiving plus $40 in expected value. So, you'd want to take as many of these challenges as possible if it cost 50 and your average 30% chance of a $300 payout." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~6:58, L220). Break-even is fee / payout: ("if you buy a challenge for 50 bucks, one out of six needs to convert to a payout, right? Because 50 * 6 is 300." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~7:19, L230); ("To cover your $100 evaluation fee, you need to make a $2,000 payment 5% of the time to break even." — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~1:07, L71); ("if you buy 100 evals, then you need five payouts of $2,000 to break even" — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~1:49, L93); ("If I buy 20 accounts, I just need one to get a payout and then I break even." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~10:52, L405). In pass-rate form: ("If your chance of passing is 25%, then you'd break even because 25% of the time you make a,000. So that's 250 in EV. Just multiply them together and then you minus 250 for each attempt." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~18:19, L550; "a,000" is $1,000), with the evaluation reframed as a 10x bet: ("How much am I actually risking? $100. What is my actual upside? $1,000. So obviously this is a 1.5R trade. And then this here is basically 10x. So if I was to win more than one out of 10, then I'd expect to make money on this given interaction." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~9:54, L311). The inequality: ("all these things will slowly increase to the point where your side of the inequality is larger than the prop firm side of the eval fee." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~25:52, L851).

The July 2026 version probability-weights the fee: ("you have a 10% chance of getting $2,000, which adds $200 to the positive side of the expected value." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~16:22, L570); ("when you don't get anything, you lose the $100 evaluation fee. So, that's minus $90. That's more than $110, which is excellent," — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~16:35, L577, medium; "more than" is the caption for "plus"). Both appear in the corpus; the September one is later and is the one `risk.eval_expected_value` should implement (p x payout - fee, +$100 on his own numbers).

Calculator outputs he reads out: ("if you spend a hundred, then you're going to make a hundred seventeen. So, well, technically like two seventeen." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~2:02, L100); ("ten accounts, right? And you can expect to make two thousand one hundred seventy eight dollars back from this expense." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~2:40, L122); per firm, ("if I trade this account, I'm going to make $225 per eval I purchase, but if I buy this one, I'm going to lose $186 per eval I purchase." — JJ, UVKVSWKFlvo ~9:20, L338). The zero-edge case: ("a person flipping a normal-sized coin has a 5.1% probability. So, a 5% probability of breaking even" — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~1:22, L80); ("if properly sized, that number could rise to 8.9% or 9.3% depending on the firm." — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~4:49, L207); ("$85 per attempt means you spend $ 100 and will receive $185 back in expected value." — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~5:08, L219). Once the win rate is raised to 45% ("let's change our success rate from 40% with a ratio of 1.5 to 45%. It's going to be a real advantage. Look at what happens with account sizing." — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~6:55, L284, risk_sizing), the most aggressive sizing in his on-screen table (near the whole account per trade, per the fact's claim rather than the caption) returns ("you recover $178" — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~7:40, L312) per $100. Where the EV comes from: ("EV is generated through getting new funded accounts because of the cost, not growing your funded accounts in profit because you're risking payout money" — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~14:48, L450); and the lever ranking: ("Increasing your payout rate and average payout size is more important than increasing your eval pass rate because the funded account is worth three times as much if you pass about one out of three evals." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~8:43, L331).

#### 6. Evolution over time

- 2026-03-08 (aBVn6IUAjnM): EV formula; the eval-fee inequality.
- 2026-03-28 (3L8xdh3oPm4): $100 / 33% = $300 cost to funded from a backtest.
- 2026-05-28 (vJuwbKibN1E): cost = fee / pass rate + activation; an evaluation is worth its fee, a fresh funded account its acquisition cost.
- 2026-06-17 (XhsbfEdJBAc): calculator base case, $100 in, $217 back; payout rate and size outrank pass rate.
- 2026-06-22 (aCOgfvL6lK8): $333 to funded and $1,000 EV as a 3x; 4x aspiration.
- 2026-07-30 (AxP-cg50TdM): $333, 16.6 cents per drawdown dollar, EV at most half the drawdown, probability-weighted fee (+$200 - $90).
- 2026-09-03 (2BtwoTCxcbc): zero-edge calibration, $85 per attempt when sized per stage.
- 2026-09-10 (0Ua-ffltO0I): the three formulas restated; one in ten at $100 is still under $1,000.
- 2026-09-22 (dJdBnSBJlgQ): EV from new funded accounts, not from growing them; 25% pass at $250 is break-even on a $1,000 funded value.
- 2026-09-29 (nvOaN6mRu-o): fee subtracted in full; include activation, resets, fees and slippage; the current formulation.
- 2026-10-04 (KHEQ5g55dQ4, PN1UKQMPb5M): $400 at 25%; $620k lifetime spend against $2M.

#### 7. Contradictions

1. Fee probability-weighted (AxP-cg50TdM L577, July) versus subtracted in full (nvOaN6mRu-o L215, September; dJdBnSBJlgQ L550). Latest: in full. The dossier's "strictly" aside and `risk.eval_expected_value` implement the July version.
2. Cost per drawdown dollar as cost / drawdown (AxP) versus drawdown / cost (0Ua-ffltO0I); reciprocals of one metric. The dossier's fee / drawdown ($750 for $4,500) uses the wrong numerator: $750 is the cost to funded.
3. Pass rates he models: 1 in 10 at $100 (0Ua-ffltO0I L206), 25% (sWJa8vRfPb8 L245), 30% (AxP), 33% (most videos and his own realised rate, KHEQ5g55dQ4 L2697). Illustrative, not one claim.
4. Break-even trader lifetime withdrawal ("about three thousand dollars" — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~2:38, L132) versus $1,000 EV per funded account (dJdBnSBJlgQ L214) and ("If you're break even on a prop firm, you can get a 3x return on your investment." — JJ, DEkzU8gqX-o ~8:58, L334); different assumed rule sets, none stated.
5. Lifetime spend: the dossier's $550,000 is the September figure; October's is about $620k.
6. An evaluation is worth its fee (vJuwbKibN1E L139) versus "You basically have no downside." (RGYbTD1j628 L72): the fee is the downside, which he calls negligible against the funded upside.

<!-- target: Section 4 -->
### From the primary corpus: risk of ruin, Kelly, bankroll, variance and the backtest-for-pass-rate method

Source as in the first block.

#### 1. Pass rate times payout rate, risk of ruin and bankroll

The chain: ("with an approval rate of 33% and a payment rate of 33%, your probability of purchasing an assessment and receiving a payment is approximately 10%." — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~2:45, L138); ("I do 0.33 * 0.33. So, it's basically one out of nine evals that I buy, I'm going to get $2,000 out of it. Each eval costs me I guess we'll say $100 on the upside. So, I'm spending 900 and then I'm withdrawing 2,000. So, with those statistics, you can have an easy 2x return." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~1:07:22, L2342); ("if I have 25% chance to pass and then 25% chance to get the payout, multiply those two together, that's your that's like 6.25% I think." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~38:46, L1378).

Ruin on a bankroll of n evaluations is (1 - p)^n: ("buy 10 assessments, your probability of failing all 10 is 0.9 to the tenth power, which is approximately 33%. That means your risk of ruin is 33% on a capital of $1,000" — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~2:55, L145; 0.9^10 = 34.9%); ("if you buy 10 evaluations, your probability of failing all of them and not getting paid is 35%." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~16:55, L588); ("If you have $1,000, you have 0.9 to the power of 10 chance of losing all your money with these two rates. If this is not a number that you like, ideally this number is less than 0.005, so half a percent, less than half a percent." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~30:01, L882); ("you can do like 0.95 to the power of maybe like 20 if you're going to buy 20 accounts, and then you'll know my chance of getting zero payouts from 20 accounts is that number." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~40:11, L1426; 0.95^20 = 35.8%); on the poker-style example ("chance of losing exponent to the power of 10 and then you get 2.8%." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~8:51, L272; 0.7^10 = 2.8%). The Chart Fanatics instance, 0.30 x 0.30 = 0.09 and 0.91^10 = 38.9%, is explained in the first block.

Thresholds: ("As soon as you start trading in funding firms, you must ensure that your risk of ruin is less than 5%. Ideally, it's less than 0.5% if you're trying to be conservative." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~6:38, L286); ("Ideally, you should aim for a risk of ruin of less than 0.5%, but of course, simulate it yourself." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~7:34, L289); ("I started with a 5% risk of ruin, now it's 0.5%." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~51:01, L1791); ("you want it to be below 5%." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:17:01, L2427). Bankroll implied: ("I would recommend at least start with a thousand just because you want your risk of ruin to be 5% or less." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~38:24, L1366); ("you want to buy about like 20 accounts or so" — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~38:31, L1370); ("5K is really good because your risk of ruin is sub 0.005%." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~30:42, L901, medium; the same number is "half a percent" at L882). His own start: ("I did start with 5,000 and I never invested more than 5,000." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~3:04, L117). The response to a small bankroll is lower risk: ("increase your likelihood of payment or your approval rate by being more conservative when your capital is lower." — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~3:21, L162). Per-trade ruin inside a funded account: ("you can solve your probability of ruin using this formula here. E raised to the power of this, this, this and this. So, you have a specific volatility that you insert into your formula" — JJ, PMGhHCjYG5c ~12:47, L548); ("This is solved using Brownian motion, a Sharpe ratio of one, and a static drawdown limit of 10%, which is even more than most of these futures firms offer. I think they usually give about 4%." — JJ, PMGhHCjYG5c ~13:01, L557); ("if you want to decrease your probability of ruin, since this is negative, you want to increase your Sharpe ratio or decrease your volatility, which is at the bottom." — JJ, PMGhHCjYG5c ~13:38, L582). The required pre-start model: ("if I start with $1,000, I have X% chance of turning it into Y dollars and I have Z% chance of losing it all." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~22:49, L655).

#### 2. Kelly, poker and bankroll rules

Kelly as he used it sizes the number of evaluations, not the trade: ("that's sort of based on the Kelly criterion, which is a mathematical way to solve for the optimal bet size in a in a rigid game where you have you like you know your win rate and you know your your risk reward." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~58:39, L2048); ("if I go in with 5,000, then based on the return that I expect to get, I would bet this specific amount. Like buy this eval because it cost 50, this one because it cost 80, this one cost 100." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~59:02, L2061); ("Now I don't really need it just because I sort of not not infinite bankroll, but I'm not going to go broke by buying eval" — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~59:32, L2078); ("You can obviously do quarter or half Kelly where you're like a little bit less aggressive than it would recommend you" — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~59:59, L2093). The coin-flip illustration: ("if you're flipping a coin and you're going to bet $100, if you if you win, you get $300. If you lose, you lose your $100." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~1:24, L66); ("if you're flipping with 100% of your net worth, though, your essentially, your risk of ruin is 50%" — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~1:39, L74); his guess at the optimal fraction, ("Probably like below 50. Maybe 25 actually." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~2:16, L94, medium; full Kelly for that bet is 1/3). Poker pot odds as the template: ("$50 a trade, 40% chance to win the trade. It's very, very similar." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~3:54, L136); ("the break even on that poker would be a 25% [clears throat] equity, but you have 40%." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~6:03, L195); bankroll rule of thumb ("I think it's like 20 times your your bankroll" — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~5:14, L189, medium). Scale changes the objective: ("If you have less than 5K, definitely go ahead optimize your ROI. Once you get past 5K, maybe 10K. It's a lot better to optimize around dollars per month." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~5:28, L185). Copy-trading ten accounts at $1,000 ("you would need probably at least $2 million net worth to do that" — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~50:29, L1773, accounts_execution).

#### 3. Variance and the binomial budget

("budget for variance binomial distribution should be pretty easy. There's calculators online for it. It can tell you your exact probability of passing 1 2 3 4 5 x out of 10 evals and then size your risk so you can keep playing." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~2:09, L88). Band at 20 attempts: ("You would expect 30% of your accounts to get a payout, but it could fall over here at like 19%. It could also go up to like 41%." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~10:14, L310). Inputs he uses: ("put in your expected value per funded account, maybe that's two or three K, and then you assume when you're given an eval purchase, you have a 10% chance of getting maybe 3K in EV or something like that." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~10:03, L406). A batch is one trade: ("I'm trading minus 25k on one trade and 48k is my profit target." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~19:40, L588) from ("we assume 30% pass, 40% of a 4K payout. That means you pass 30. 12 reach the payouts of $4,000 gives you 48K in payouts." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~19:17, L577); ("if half of them if you have a bad variance streak you lose money. So, it is still totally possible to lose money even when you're doing it right." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~20:16, L605). The payout-per-account distribution: ("It's more like a 50% chance at two payouts and a 50% chance at zero payouts." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~6:14, L207). At scale ("there's not really a statistical likeliness that you experience bad variance over spending like $20,000 a month." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~9:41, L392).

#### 4. The backtest-for-pass-rate method

("Let me mark this as 48. This is going to be for 50k, 53. What you should do when you're back testing is do this sort of thing. Start here. One trade per day. Just one trade per day." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~32:16, L1098). End-of-day trailing: ("You win a thousand. Now you're right here. So it looked like this because you ended the day up a thousand. Now this is no longer your drawdown. Now your drawdown is 49,000 because the way end of day trailing drawdown works." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~32:58, L1121); ("if you win, you bring your drawdown up to 49. If you win again, you hit 52, you bring your drawdown up to 50. Drawdown stops trailing at 50." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~33:45, L1147). Output: ("if it's $100 for the prop firm and you're passing 33% of them, meaning you're hitting 53 before the trailing drawdown, uh 33% of the time you win, then this would be $300." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~34:01, L1156). Rules: ("pretend you're on an eval. Pretend you have that daily loss or that max loss." — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~12:17, L434); ("Make sure you trail that draw down and then you can literally see what your own pass rate is. If your pass rate is it's like 20%. Well, then do something else." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~13:47, L506); ("Run retrospective tests and prove to yourselves that you are profitable in the funding company environment before touching a real account." — JJ, KhooqEQK9bA ~0:35, L53). Pass-rate floors: ("anything above 20% makes money" — JJ, r5AhmGaFujU ~14:27, L460); ("I drop off by about 3 to 4% when I use the 40% one. Nothing you can do about it. It's just extra rules." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~5:30, L234); E8 ("Usually I'll pass like one out of four evals, one out of three evals on E8 specifically because they have 40% consistency." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~26:27, L870). His live backtest check: ("to lose a 50k account, you have to lose $2,000, which is 4%. And as you can see, we never hit 4%." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~46:52, L1268). The bookkeeping: ("Record every trade you make, your success rate, your expected value, and your risk-reward ratio. Then you can calculate your approval rate, your probability of payment, the size of your payments, the expected value you generate with the funded account, how much it cost you to obtain that account" — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~11:40, L481). Proof of edge: ("if you spent more than 5k and have a positive return, then you know you will be profitable in the funding firm environment." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~5:43, L226).

#### 6. Evolution over time

- 2026-03-08 (aBVn6IUAjnM): ruin defined, the $100/$300 coin flip, a 20 buy-in poker rule, a $25k starting bankroll with $50 evaluations (L777).
- 2026-03-28 and 2026-04-03 (3L8xdh3oPm4, Lep139B5EbU): the backtest frame with EOD trailing at one trade per day; optimise for the firm's pass rate, not live profit.
- 2026-06-10 (MVP7X-3v8xk): his own backtest never drew down 4%.
- 2026-06-22 (aCOgfvL6lK8): Kelly for eval purchases, now dropped; ruin 5% at the start, 0.5% now; the 33% x 33% x $2,000 model.
- 2026-08-06 to 2026-08-27 (S8fSNbPFuLA, MtBZeAkYH8Q, PMGhHCjYG5c, 4BXpI-hYqe0): ruin below 5%, ideally 0.5%; streak arithmetic; the exponential ruin formula; the $5k proof.
- 2026-09-06 and 2026-09-15 (KhooqEQK9bA, sWJa8vRfPb8): retrospective tests in the firm environment; trail the drawdown; change the approach below about 20%.
- 2026-09-22 and 2026-09-24 (dJdBnSBJlgQ, VY-bO35-tjE): ruin under 0.005; $5,000 start; ROI below $5k and dollars per month above; binomial inputs of 10% per eval for about $3k.
- 2026-09-29 (nvOaN6mRu-o): binomial variance budget; 0.7^10; model exact ruin and EV before starting; the current method.
- 2026-10-04 (KHEQ5g55dQ4): ruin below 5%; the 9% / 91% chain and 0.91^10.

#### 7. Contradictions

1. Ruin threshold 5% (KHEQ5g55dQ4 L2427, 2026-10-04) versus 0.5% (4BXpI-hYqe0 L289; dJdBnSBJlgQ L882). Reconciled by his own statement that 5% is the starting figure and 0.5% the scaled one (aCOgfvL6lK8 L1791).
2. 0.9^10 stated as 33% (MtBZeAkYH8Q L145) and 35% (AxP-cg50TdM L588); actual 34.9%.
3. Kelly: the dossier computes full Kelly on the drawdown allowance (23%) as his per-trade fraction; he only ever describes Kelly as sizing the eval purchases from a bankroll and says he no longer uses it.
4. One trade per day in his pass-rate backtest (3L8xdh3oPm4 L1098) versus the many trades a day he reports live; the backtest frame is a method, not a cadence claim.
5. "there's not really a statistical likeliness that you experience bad variance over spending like $20,000 a month" (VY-bO35-tjE L392) versus "it is still totally possible to lose money even when you're doing it right" (dJdBnSBJlgQ L605) at 100 evaluations a month.
6. The ruin formula he shows assumes a static 10% drawdown (PMGhHCjYG5c L557) while every rule set he trades trails; he presents it as an approximation only.

## 5. Multi-account operation

| Item | What the sources say | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Account count | 20-30 (his X posts), 40 ("runs approximately 40 accounts", Road to $1M Ep. 2; in his own words "if you're running 40 accounts at a time. `fpt/portfolio.py` reproduces the economics under his own operating rules
(defaults since the corpus integration): about 20 signals a day routed one
per account in rotation, evaluations attacked at target / (2 x 1.5) per
trade, funded accounts at $1,000, 22 trading days a month, payouts only
after five winning days of $150 or more and at 50% of profit per request
under each firm's cap. With the cap-feasible 45-account mix of Section 9
(Topstep 5, Tradeify Growth 5, MFFU Pro 3, Lucid 5, Alpha 5, Apex 20, E8
2) at 54% / 1.5R (1,000 sims, seed 0; `python -m fpt.cli portfolio
--account topstep_100k:5 ... --eval-risk-mode two_trade`), the six-month
median net cash is about $292k, with a 5th percentile near $226k, every
path positive, monthly net rising to roughly $70-86k by months four to six
and 33-44 accounts funded; with fixed $1,000 evaluation risk the median is
about $193k. His own reported pace ("$105,700 in 3 weeks" on about 40
accounts, "six figures a month") sits inside this range, which is the first
calibration in this project that lands on his numbers without special
pleading. The earlier copy-trading runs (every account taking 1.5 signals a
day) gave $1.0-1.2M medians and are an upper bound, not his operation.
Seven of the twelve presets (Topstep x3, MyFundedFutures Rapid, Tradeify
Growth and Select, Alpha Standard) are backed by the firms' help centers;
the rest are third-party placeholders (Section 9).

**Operation details from the `accounts` angle (34 facts).** Payouts by
firm from his $1.6M breakdown: Topstep ~$292,000; E8 $222,122 (the one
firm-issued "Certificate of Performance" found); Tradeify ~$180,000 (the captions' "TradiFi"; the earlier research read it as "Funded Engineer")
(an unnamed firm's dashboard on jjwebinar.com shows 48 payouts totalling
$179,938, $123,901 disbursed as of April 2026, largest $46,433, rolling
monthly payouts of $47,300 plus $17,250 lump sums; the matching total is the
only basis for linking it to the "Funded Engineer" line); Funded Next $129,500; Lucid ~$105,000; MyFundedFutures
~$92,000; Alpha Futures ~$75,000; Apex ~$60,000; Hola Prime ~$55,000 (the captions' "Oola Prime"; earlier read as "Bulwark Prime").
Routine: "trade each account one at a time and get through all of his
accounts every day, though he sometimes copy trades two together"; "you
should not copy trade until you're making like 20k" (verbatim: "Sometimes I copy two trades together, but you shouldn't do copy trading until you're making around 20k a month", KN7j6NXXAio L105, 2026-07-25; the same threshold is $10,000 of bankroll in March and May 2026, 50k a month on 2026-06-06, 30K a month on 2026-07-30, 20-30 thousand a month on 2026-08-09 and 35k a month for copying two evaluations under $150 on 2026-09-24); once funded "he
cycles one trade across multiple funded accounts"; a conservative
evaluation uses "a minimum of two trades, but most likely four". He buys
evaluations in bulk ("160 evals for 250 bucks each"), says he has spent
$550,000 on evaluations in total, and argues that "one trade a day ...
22 trades a month ... approximately 3K a month" cannot reach $100k, which
is why the account count matters more than the edge. He risks
"different on separate firms and accounts" ([jjwebinar.com](https://jjwebinar.com/));
the 365-day backtester's reading is that "his real edge is understanding the
prop firms and having a different risk strategy per prop firm" and that the
method is "optimized for prop firms and less effective on live accounts"
(**3rd**). He has published a calculator, PropFirmEV,
that replays a trader's statistics through each firm's rulebook thousands
of times at one trade per day; `fpt/portfolio.py` is the same idea.
Monthly examples from his posts: a $48K month with a $45K payout; four
June payouts totalling $25,000; $90,000 made toward a $175,000 record-month
target. He also
says he picks which account takes a given setup "based on the market
displacement and points away from fair price", and sizes by "max EV then
mess with variance, but keep variance low to start". His cost-per-drawdown
rule of thumb: above about $0.50 of fee per $1 of drawdown a firm is
"generally not profitable" to use; verbatim: "if your cost per loss margin is greater than 50 cents, you won't be profitable at that specific site" and "your cost per loss margin should be less than 50 cents. Otherwise, you shouldn't even be operating" (AxP-cg50TdM L194, L210; "loss margin" is the caption's word for drawdown). Note the arithmetic gap: the per-firm
figures he lists ($292K + ~$50K old Topstep dashboard + $222K + $180K +
$129.5K + $105K + $92K + $75K + $60K + $55K) sum to roughly $1.26M against
the $1.6M headline of the same video; the remainder is unexplained.
Known from the transcripts: accounts are mostly 150k ("Some are 50k, but mostly 150k", 7KJOh2NF-lQ L954); the evaluation budget is "160 evaluations at $250 each" a month (AxP-cg50TdM L915); cumulative evaluation spend went from "About 200 to 250k" (2026-03-28, 3L8xdh3oPm4 L86) to $550,000 (September 2026, dJdBnSBJlgQ L31) to "probably 620,000 maybe a bit less" (2026-10-04, PN1UKQMPb5M L515). Unknown still: the copier product, if any, and spend versus
payouts per month.

### From the primary corpus: the tier list and the selection tests

**1. Rules in his own words**

*1.1 The premise: beat the firm, not the market.* ("You have to beat the funding company, not the market." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~0:46, L58); ("when you're given a set of specific rules, there is a statistically optimal way to approach said problem." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~2:26, L143); ("if five different firms exist with five different rule structures, your strategy must be optimal on one of those five. It's up to you to figure out which one it is." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~6:37, L313); ("there is no best prop firm. It is the best one for you if you have a different strategy, a different risk to reward than me." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~7:22, L344); ("stop asking which firm is best and start asking which firm is going to pay you the most in expected value, or which firm is going to get you to a funded account that for the cheapest amount of cost." — JJ, 0Ua-ffltO0I ~7:27, L347); ("It is a complete information game, which is amazing and I think it's why I've made so much money doing it." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~13:53, L410). The objective function: ("Your sole objective in a funding firm environment is to generate the highest possible volume of withdrawals, while keeping your expenses reasonably low, of course." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~2:28, L116); ("With a prop firm, your goal is to have this payout be bigger than this, expenses." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~8:57, L339). The firm's side: ("prop firms make money when people fail challenges. They have money in when people fail, and they pay out to winning traders. So, prop firms want you to lose so they can make money. They don't copy trade, they don't book your trades, or anything like that." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~9:28, L357); ("make sure you know the rules, because the rules are the way prop firms make money. So, if you understand the rules, you can build a strategy and tailor your trading strategy specifically to make the most amount of money where the rules aren't restricting you." — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~24:00, L830); ("I realized they're so inefficient. You can make so much money on a prop firm if you're approaching it optimally." — JJ, 1CEAgRUNZ4c ~17:19, L647).

*1.2 The April 2026 ranking and his reasons.* The only tier list in the corpus is 5RzMu2B2E_0 (2026-04-12). The extracted facts carry explicit letters for S (Tradeify, Lucid, Topstep), A (Apex; FundedNext by "why it's not the S tier") and B (Bulenox); for the other firms they carry his reasons but not the letter. ("you're a newer trader, just start on these three. There's no reason to use anything else other than these three. I know lots of influencers promoting Alpha. Just there's no reason to use it. You could get the exact same account with better rules, better profit split, and cheaper, and faster payouts on Lucid." — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~22:28, L779); ("Topstep, Lucid, and TradiFi are personally the prop firms that have paid me out the most, and they have very good prices as well." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~30:23, L1096); ("Topstep is 100% the best site in terms of payout stacking. I also have Trade the Five, Funded Next, E8, and a bunch of others." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~0:27, L39). Reasons per firm: Tradeify, the live salary program, simple rules and Select daily or weekly payouts (Section 9 blocks); Topstep, the $5K cap, winning-days-only payout rule, 90% split and $4,500 of drawdown on the 150K, with the live program as the caveat; Lucid, 90%, cheaper and faster than Alpha, first bought when it appeared (("I bought my first one September 8th, I believe with Lucid" — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~31:12, L1123)), with three plans (("Lucid has a daily. Lucid has a flex. Luca has a pro." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~17:38, L640)); MyFundedFutures, the same 50K rules as Lucid and Topstep but dearer and 80% (("They have the 50K Flex, which is the exact same rules as the Lucid 50K Flex and just the standard Topstep 50K. The only reason it's a bit lower than those two is just the cost. It's almost like, I don't know, 60% more. It's like $110, maybe even more now." — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~19:29, L679)); Apex, A tier for 20 cheap end-of-day accounts despite the daily loss limit, buffer and consistency rule; FundedNext, daily $1.2K payouts and variety (("they have so much variety. They have three different account types. Uh they have bolt, legacy, and rapid." — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~3:35, L146)) but 80%; Bulenox, B for three accounts and a $1.5K cap; Alpha Futures (("just the problem overall with Alpha Features is it's one of the worst sites in general in terms of expected value. Uh and that's just from my personal experience. Like it's the one that I have one of the lowest returns on, except for Take Profit Trader, but I'll get there." — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~4:50, L188)); Take Profit Trader, lowest return, 80%, intraday trailing; Elite Trader Funding, $1K caps; GoFunded, 80% and payout windows; E8, capped now but (("the return on your investment with E8 can be incredibly good if you're a good trader." — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~16:48, L589)); DayTraders, deletes 75% on transfer; Funded Futures Family, banned him; The Trading Pit, a $2K buffer and about 80%. Firms that appear later without a tier: Vest Markets (daily payouts, Oct 2026), Hola Prime (pays within an hour, Aug 2026), Goat Funded Futures (("we will proceed to request this $2,000 payment from Goat Funded Futures. There it is, pending. And then I had a second account, so I qualify for the second account as well." — JJ, q8PXXvY9lS0 ~4:26, L177)), Blue Guardian, TradeDay, FTMO, a "funded seat" live product; for contract size (("But funded accounts with higher contract sizing would be E8, FTMO, and funded next." — JJ, r5AhmGaFujU ~16:42, L527)). Scale-stage firms he names for a student: ("So, I would add back in My Funded Futures and probably use the pro accounts right now. I would use the 50K on the pros. There's no No no point in the 150K just because the return is a lot higher on the the 50." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~6:37, L276).

*1.3 The cost tests.* ("You can usually tell, or assume, that if your cost per loss margin is greater than 50 cents, you won't be profitable at that specific site." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~4:58, L194); ("Therefore, your cost per loss margin should be less than 50 cents. Otherwise, you shouldn't even be operating." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~5:27, L210); ("if you're trading prop firm challenges, you want to buy the cheapest ones as possible if you have an edge because you want your risk of ruin to reduce as much as possible so that you're able to realize more of your edge in the long run." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~2:31, L102). Cost to a funded account and its expected payout: ("So the cost, your pass rate, which you can back test, divide it and then you get the average cost of a funded account. From that, you can know if you're profitable based on more statistics like your average payout and your payout chance." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~34:18, L1165); ("Now, you know, if I get a funded account, my expected payout is $1,000. Now, can I get to the funded account for less than $1,000? Hopefully." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~1:55, L93); ("So, we'll just assume you have a 50% chance of a $2,000 payout. The expected value of that is just 50% * 2,000, which is a,000. So, now you know the positive side of the expected value is $1,000. But how much did the account cost? 400. That is why we get a $600 profit per funded account." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~7:09, L275) (the caption's "a,000" is $1,000); with a second payout ("You could also have a 50% chance of 2,000 plus maybe you have a 50% chance to get a 1k payout after that. So it' be 0.5 time 2k plus 0.5 * 0.5 * 1k." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~8:02, L306). Two equal-EV firms and budget: ("Maybe there's a site where I'm trying to get a 20% chance at a 10K payout. Maybe there's another site where I'm trying to get a 50% chance at a 4K payout. Both of these funded accounts generate me 2K in EV." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~32:42, L958); ("So, both of these accounts may be the same amount of money, but this one is a much lower probability of losing. So, if you have a lower budget, you would prefer to do one of these." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~32:53, L963). The four tracked statistics: ("There are only four main things you really need to track when dealing with funding companies. First, your assessment success rate." — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~5:54, L265); ("Once you're funded, track the exact same statistics. How often do you win on your funded account trades? But then, instead of just an approval rate, what's your withdrawal rate?" — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~6:18, L281); ("By multiplying your withdrawal rate by the size of the withdrawals, you can find out the expected value of your funded account" — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~6:27, L287); ("This can be applied to different companies with different rules, and you can automatically see if your strategy with your success rate would be profitable under those new sets of rules." — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~6:36, L293). Where his own numbers are negative: ("even losing money on Topstep. I'm losing money on some MFF accounts, right? So, why would you buy those when you have the same strategy which can literally make you like 300 more dollars." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~16:50, L588); ("So I would never buy the MFX 25K to only make 1,300 when I could buy this and make like five times as much." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~49:26, L1336); and the opportunity cost of small firms ("I prefer not to add little niche sites that could make me like 2K EV per month, but obviously I could." — JJ, 1CEAgRUNZ4c ~20:23, L756). The selector he publishes: ("You insert your trading stats, total wins, total trades, wins, average win, average loss, calculate EV, and then based on your stats, it will tell you the optimal prop you can trade with." — JJ, UVKVSWKFlvo ~9:11, L333); the fallback ("simulate It's four different prop firms, all with different rules. One of them has to work best for you. And then just choose and use the firm that worked the best for you." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~17:10, L500).

March 2026 (aBVn6IUAjnM): choose by rules and style, buy the cheapest challenges, Topstep for home runs, consistency accounts for small wins. May 2026: wsKx7L9EJys lists Alpha Futures Premium 50K among four same-rule-set accounts to layer in, and vJuwbKibN1E reports losing money per eval on Topstep and some MFF accounts. June 2026: Topstep ("Topstep is 100% the best site in terms of payout stacking." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~0:27, L39); Topstep, Lucid and Tradeify have paid the most (aCOgfvL6lK8). July 2026: the cost-per-drawdown test and the account-value framing (AxP-cg50TdM). August 2026: the four tracked statistics (MtBZeAkYH8Q); 20 trusted firms (S8fSNbPFuLA). September 2026: EV per funded account worked example (sWJa8vRfPb8); equal-EV firms and budget (dJdBnSBJlgQ); niche firms skipped (1CEAgRUNZ4c); simulate across four firms (nvOaN6mRu-o). October 2026 (PN1UKQMPb5M, latest): "the 10 best" firms with continual replacement, Vest Markets added, Alpha funded accounts resumed. No later video re-ranks the firms; the April list is the only ranking and its Alpha and Take Profit Trader verdicts are the ones his later behaviour departs from.

**4. Contradictions**

Within his statements: (a) Alpha Futures: ("Just there's no reason to use it." — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~22:28, L779) (Apr 2026) against ("I recommend doing the MyFunded Futures Flex 50K, Topstep Standard 50K, Alpha Futures Premium 50K" — JJ, wsKx7L9EJys ~7:26, L253) (May 2026) and resumed Alpha funded trading (Oct 2026); (b) Topstep: negative per-eval EV in his own stats (May 2026) against S tier (Apr), ("Topstep is 100% the best site in terms of payout stacking." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~0:27, L39) (June) and ("I think Topstep is one of the worst live account programs in general" — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~13:56, L491) (May), which coexist only if the funded stage carries the value and the eval and live stages do not; (c) Take Profit Trader: among the lowest returns and intraday trailing (Apr) against June-July payouts of $9,600 and $4,400 and same-night live withdrawals. With the dossier: Section 5's "Firms" row lists the 17 names from his X post; the corpus adds firms he trades or names (Take Profit Trader, Goat Funded Futures, Hola Prime, Vest Markets, Blue Guardian, TradeDay, The Trading Pit, GoFunded Futures, Elite Trader Funding, DayTraders, Funded Futures Family, FTMO, a "funded seat" product), and none of these facts names "Funded Engineer". Section 5 paraphrases the cost-per-drawdown rule with the words "generally not profitable", which are not in the corpus; his words are ("you won't be profitable at that specific site." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~4:58, L194). The dossier's cap-feasible mix gives Alpha 5 and Apex 20 of 45 accounts; the only list he tells a newer trader to start on is three firms (5RzMu2B2E_0 L779), and his own mix is ("the 10 best profms at all time" — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~23:02, L716) with no per-firm counts.

### From the primary corpus: onboarding a firm, how many firms, and how the rules change the trading

**1. Rules in his own words**

*1.1 Onboarding a firm.* ("So, what I do when I find a new firm is I try it out one account only and then if they pay me then I'll invest more." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~31:22, L1129); ("Just trying my best to look online, YouTube, see if they have public presence and then stay away from the scams." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~31:29, L1133); ("So, I like to get into these new little firms and I'll only trade one account at a time." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~48:52, L1566); his own start ("So I started with 150k to get a payout." — JJ, 1CEAgRUNZ4c ~17:32, L655); the research load ("spending my entire day simulating, researching, adding new prop firms, just wondering if a new firm is going to pay. Got to research them, research the rules." — JJ, 1CEAgRUNZ4c ~18:37, L693); ("I bought quite a few accounts because I was going to trade with four new funding companies that I'm testing." — JJ, KhooqEQK9bA ~15:13, L637). Help center first: ("most failed evaluations are not due to poor operations. These are rule violations. Before trading with a funding firm, you should read their entire help center, because I assure you there are hidden rules you didn't see." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~1:58, L99); ("just go check the help center, please. It is 100% worth it. It'll take 15 minutes." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~7:44, L283). Not by reputation or by whom you follow: ("Don't choose it based on which one you think is the best because they have the best payout policy or they have a really good reputation or you see your favorite trader trading with them. You need to go through the prop firms, read the rules, and trade which one resonates best with you and your personal trading." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~16:12, L541). Account size follows firm trust: ("Yeah, definitely firm dependent. Like you don't want to trade 150ks on the smaller sites that might scam or might send you live." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~21:47, L849); ("there's some little weird niche sites that will at some point in the future that you don't you don't even heard of yet um that you want to just trade for the highest ROI possible." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~21:55, L854); ("The first one is add a few more sites on 50K accounts. Just because 150Ks are going to be pretty high variance still." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~6:18, L264); ("You need a strategy that works for 50k accounts, then you'll probably have to change it for 150k accounts." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~26:26, L902).

*1.2 How many firms.* ("I just trade with the 10 best profms at all time. And there's way more than 10 prop firms, as I'm sure you know. So, I just always add a new prop firm. Um, so I'm able to do numbers like this every single month for the last 18 months." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~23:02, L716); ("But now, I have a specific list of 20 trusted firms, the only 20 I will spend my money on. I already know the optimal strategy, risk, and take profit for all these firms." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~10:34, L438); ("Obviously, there's more than 25 different prop firms. Each proper probably has three different plans you can choose from and three different account sizes you choose from. So you multiply 25 by 3x3 and that is a lot of risk managements that I would need to show you." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~0:29, L43); plan variants he optimises separately (("Like Topstep, well, top only has one plan, but like Tradeify has some instant funage. They have a growth. They have a select." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~17:38, L640)).

*1.3 How the rules change the trading.* ("each prop firm there must exist a an optimal risk uh and it's different [snorts] across each firm because they have different rules, so I'll trade a different account based on the sort of move I'm expecting to see." — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~11:31, L409); ("So what I do is optimize according to the rules of the funding company, and I execute it in a specific account to take advantage of all that expected value." — JJ, MtBZeAkYH8Q ~5:17, L240); ("combined with risk and reward targets optimized for the funding company environment. That is, basically the optimal size based on the set of rules they give me and the expected value of my account." — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~9:48, L390); ("this changes as the funding environment evolves , their rules change, I'm moved to real accounts, and evaluation fees increase ; All of that is going to change constantly." — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~9:57, L396); ("And the reason I've been able to secure $1.6 million in payments from funding firms with only a 2% advantage over break-even is that I've perfectly matched the size to these firms' specific environments." — JJ, 4IGbxmKJ4BU ~1:18, L84). The adaptations the corpus records: evaluation winners capped at $1,500 by the 50% rule; large targets reserved for firms without a consistency rule or a strict live policy; consistency accounts for small-win trading and Topstep for home runs (("if you're someone who likes to go for home runs, risk high risk high reward, Topstep is a good firm for you because they have a 5K max payout." — JJ, aBVn6IUAjnM ~16:31, L551)); daily-payout accounts taken at the maximum as soon as eligible; 20 Apex end-of-day accounts because they are cheap; sim balances held under the live threshold; ("On a live account your goal is equity. On a prop firm your goal is to not have drawdown." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~16:29, L573); ("Every company has a different strategy, okay? Because it's optimal to do different things in different companies since they have different rules." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~33:02, L1120); ("your live account strategy will most likely lose on prop firms because of how they structure the rules. That means you would either need to change your strategy or you need to change your prop firm approach." — JJ, XhsbfEdJBAc ~10:03, L377). Rule-set families for scaling: ("Once you're past $10,000, layer in more firms and take the exact same rule set. All of these have pretty much the exact same rule set. I recommend doing the MyFunded Futures Flex 50K, Topstep Standard 50K, Alpha Futures Premium 50K, and TradiFy Select 50K." — JJ, wsKx7L9EJys ~7:26, L253); ("If your tradeify works best that I can get you a list of sites that have very, very similar rules to that and then we would assume that your skill there would transfer over as well." — JJ, r5AhmGaFujU ~10:38, L347; medium); the student process ("Every time he added a new company, he worked with me. We studied the rules and developed a plan to approach that specific company in a statistically optimal way. Then we diversified the companies and repeated the process every month." — JJ, xNeBEqv06k0 ~7:17, L296). Why he will not publish per-account settings: ("if everyone does it, Lucid Flex is going to have to change the rules." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~11:32, L428).

**3. How the statements evolved**

The one-account rule for a new firm is stated in June 2026 (aCOgfvL6lK8) and October 2026 (KHEQ5g55dQ4, latest), the ramp to five funded accounts only in October. His own first purchases were 150K accounts "to get a payout" (1CEAgRUNZ4c, Sept 2026, recalling 2025); by September 2026 he tells a student to keep 150Ks off small firms and to add firms on 50K accounts (VY-bO35-tjE). The help-center instruction appears in May 2026 (wsKx7L9EJys, vJuwbKibN1E) and August 2026 (4BXpI-hYqe0). Firm counts: 20 trusted (Aug 2026), 25+ exist with three plans and three sizes each (Sept 2026), "the 10 best" in rotation (Oct 2026, latest). The per-firm-optimal-risk statements run from April 2026 (Lep139B5EbU) through September 2026 (2BtwoTCxcbc) unchanged; the same-rule-set scaling family is from May 2026 (wsKx7L9EJys) and is not repeated later, and the student session of October 2026 replaces it with ("a list of sites that have very, very similar rules to that" — JJ, r5AhmGaFujU ~10:38, L347; medium) for whichever firm works best.

**4. Contradictions**

Within his statements: (a) ("you don't want to trade 150ks on the smaller sites that might scam or might send you live." — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~21:47, L849) (Sept 2026) against ("I started with 150k to get a payout." — JJ, 1CEAgRUNZ4c ~17:32, L655) at his first firms, early versus later practice; (b) ("the 10 best profms at all time" — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~23:02, L716) (Oct 2026) against ("a specific list of 20 trusted firms" — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~10:34, L438) (Aug 2026), reconcilable as a shortlist inside a trusted list but not stated as such; (c) ("I'll only trade one account at a time." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~48:52, L1566) at new firms against ("I bought quite a few accounts because I was going to trade with four new funding companies that I'm testing." — JJ, KhooqEQK9bA ~15:13, L637), which is one account per firm across four firms only if read charitably. With the dossier: Section 5's account-count row (20-30, 40, 45+) can add his own ("if you're running 40 accounts at a time. So, you could even run 30 accounts." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~1:06:22, L2308) (June 2026), 20 Apex accounts and the 22-account teaching model; Section 9's claim that the cap-feasible mix matches the "45+ accounts" is the dossier's inference; his own statements on counts are "the 10 best" firms and "20 trusted firms" without per-firm numbers, except Apex 20. `PortfolioConfig.routing` offers copy or round-robin; his statement here is per-firm account selection by expected move, which is neither.

### From the primary corpus: the account stack and the firms, dated

Scope for the four blocks below: the 250 facts in `extracted/_by_group/accounts_execution.json` (234 spoken by JJ, 5 by hosts, 11 by students or guests; 229 high confidence, 21 medium), plus the eval-spend facts the brief names, which sit in `eval_risk_math.json`, `results.json` and `profile_other.json`. Upload dates come from `transcript-index.md`. Medium-confidence facts are flagged in prose.

**1. The stack in his own words**

*How many accounts.* The earliest count is 40, on 2026-03-28: ("I have 40 accounts, I'm copy trading five at once, and I want to get maximum exposure to the edge before it dies." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~8:16, L305). By June he describes the stack by firm: ("I'm trading on 10 firms right now, all 150k accounts trying to have all five funded active at once on all of the firms. So, on a bad streak, I lose four accounts if I lose four in a row." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~23:49, L816). On 2026-08-06: ("diversifying across more than 45 accounts at once" — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~10:52, L450). The 2026-08-23 video gives two figures: ("I personally might log in early because I'm trying to complete 20 accounts." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~37:16, L1263) and ("I prefer to have a 1% advantage that I apply to 40 accounts a day, and that generates six figures a month for me." — JJ, 74CRg-mID5c ~38:47, L1313). September: ("Obviously, I can't record while I'm literally trading 17 plus 22 accounts in one reversion." — JJ, HlWSP7ajgpQ ~4:24, L169; 2026-09-13), ("if you don't have 50 accounts you should not trade the same way as I do." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~3:35, L144; 2026-09-20), ("when I'm trying to get through 50 accounts." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~13:51, L470) and, to a student, ("if you want to be trading 50 accounts, which is where we need to be for a 100K amount" — JJ, VY-bO35-tjE ~16:10, L637; 2026-09-24). The two latest videos (2026-10-04): ("if I'm trying to get through 40 accounts, I want to spread my risk across all of my accounts" — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~6:36, L274), ("but thankfully I have 45 accounts I'm ready to enter again" — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:00:03, L1908) and the only line that splits funded from evaluation: ("unless you have like 40 funded like I do, then you wouldn't need to layer funds." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~28:38, L883). His own firm count over time: ("I'll tell you the exact five firms I run this on later to scale" — JJ, wsKx7L9EJys ~2:41, L106; 2026-05-10, medium); ("I was probably trading on seven firms at a time." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~1:04:05, L2231, describing the period he was stuck at 75k a month); ten from June (L2218 above; ("I just operate in them with like 10 different companies." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~32:28, L1101, medium)); ("I'm still mainly active on like 10 right now. Firms are added for what they offer: ("add on the A tier where you're getting something that you don't have on the S tier, if that makes sense. Like the reason you would add Apex is so you can have 20 accounts." — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~5:22, L206; 2026-04-12); ("So, I have funded next for the daily payouts whenever I'm doing good every day. Live accounts: ("I'm also working on live accounts right now with Topstep, Lucid, Tradeify, a bunch of other sites, including Take Profit Trader" — JJ, CzxSgYujDxs ~26:04, L860); ("they gave me a live account and now I'm still trading on that live account" — JJ, 5RzMu2B2E_0 ~11:38, L416, Lucid, since October 2025).

*Evaluations versus funded.* The live transition caps how far he grows a funded account: ("I don't want to grow these accounts too large because I know once I hit the live account that it's going back to zero." — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~15:17, L536; 2026-05-28); the trigger is roughly ("you're probably not going to go live unless you don't have like 25, maybe 30k in your account" — JJ, vJuwbKibN1E ~13:22, L472, medium). A live account is expendable next to the evaluation pipeline: ("I don't mind losing it because I have five new assessments waiting for me to trade as soon as I finish" — JJ, pwO9IAu7FUQ ~13:42, L478; 2026-08-02) and ("basically I was very aggressive because I wanted to lose the account to go back to the evaluation account" — JJ, pwO9IAu7FUQ ~17:16, L595). Daily mixes he reports: ("there were six funded accounts and nine evaluations, and I literally did nothing more than identify my trend." — JJ, MIWsdldzJgQ ~7:44, L285, overnight with limit orders); ("I traded 35 evaluations on this day. **3. Evolution, dated**

March 2026: 40 accounts. April: five FundedNext Advanced 60k accounts, Apex purchases barred, a Lucid live account. May: "five firms" (medium). June: seven then ten firms, all 150k, five funded per firm as the goal; Tradeify capped at three after the $30,000 day. July: 40 accounts. August: "more than 45"; a new firm tested with 50k accounts. September: 50 accounts, ten of about 25 firms active, 10-12 firms have moved him live. October 4 (latest): 40-45 accounts, "40 funded". The current state is 40 funded plus a rolling evaluation pool across about 10 firms, mostly 150k accounts.

**4. Contradictions**

Within his statements: 20 versus 40 accounts in one video (74CRg-mID5c L1263 vs L1313); "I trade 10 to 12 firms at a time" (1CEAgRUNZ4c L751) three days after "I don't trade more than 10 at a time" (VY-bO35-tjE L877); 40, 45 and 50 accounts within three weeks (KHEQ5g55dQ4 L274, L1908; l6iq0ljhxIo L144), which the "infinite SIM funded accounts" line explains as a floating evaluation pool. Against the dossier: the "20-30 (his X posts)" count has no transcript support; "per-firm account counts (he gives none)" is wrong for E8, Tradeify, Apex, FundedNext and Lucid; the Section 9 note that the official caps "sum to about 45" is consistent with his "Only five accounts funded at a time in one place" (0uAQUHEB_L8 L561) but he is explicit that Tradeify is three by choice and E8 three by the firm.

**5. Replication parameters**

- `PortfolioConfig.accounts`: 40-50 accounts across ~10 firms, 5 funded per firm (E8 3, Tradeify 3, Apex up to 20), mostly 150k - changed (default is 10 x topstep_100k); aCOgfvL6lK8 L2218; 0uAQUHEB_L8 L561; PN1UKQMPb5M L883; l6iq0ljhxIo L144; VY-bO35-tjE L324; CzxSgYujDxs L81; 5RzMu2B2E_0 L206.
- `FirmRules.account_size`: 150k as the working size, 50k for new-firm tests - changed (presets are 100k); 7KJOh2NF-lQ L954; pwO9IAu7FUQ L100.
- `funded_cap_per_firm` (plain): 5 as the norm, 3 at E8 and Tradeify, 20 at Apex - confirmed against Section 9; 0uAQUHEB_L8 L561; VY-bO35-tjE L324; CzxSgYujDxs L81; 5RzMu2B2E_0 L206.
- `reserve_passed_evals_per_firm` (plain): 2-3 passed evaluations held in reserve per firm (stated for E8 only) - new; VY-bO35-tjE L324, L329.

### From the primary corpus: evaluation spend, replacement, the held-back account, platform and routine

**1. In his own words**

*Spend on evaluations* (facts from the other group files, as the brief asks). 2026-03-28: ("Uh how much did I spend on evals? About 200 to 250k." — JJ, 3L8xdh3oPm4 ~1:38, L86). The monthly budget, 2026-07-30: ("To earn 140 in payments, you need to spend 40,000. That will leave you with 100,000. So, how am I going to spend 40,000 now? Okay, I'm going to do 160 evaluations at $250 each." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~26:49, L915); ("So, you need 160 evaluations per month to spend 40,000. So my goal is not to earn 100,000 a month. My goal is, how am I going to spend 40,000 a month?" — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~26:37, L908); ("just think of 640 trades per month in evaluations." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~27:57, L952). Cumulative: ("I have received $1.8 million in payments from funding firms and have spent $550,000 on appraisals" — JJ, 2BtwoTCxcbc ~9:07, L365; 2026-09-03); ("I spent $550,000 on profit valuations, but it resulted in $1,931,000 worth of profit payouts after the profit split." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~0:02, L31; 2026-09-22); ("That buys me 2,200 valuations." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~3:12, L121, at an assumed $250 average). Latest, 2026-10-04: ("spend probably 620,000 maybe a bit less. Haven't clarified with my accountant. I'll probably do that end of month. We're at 550 last month." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~16:17, L515). His KPI order: ("First, eval spend. That is the most important. Second, number of payouts in dollars." — JJ, sWJa8vRfPb8 ~14:54, L545). Spend is driven by the firm count: ("trying to have all five funded active at once on all of the firms. So it does take about it does take a lot of spend." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~1:03:41, L2218).

*Replacement and renewal.* Failed accounts are replaced as a matter of course: ("Many of my accounts haven't won either. They reach maximum loss from time to time. That's perfectly normal. Now I have the capital to replace them." — JJ, J8qXxtFFpGg ~1:23, L82); ("I'm operating aggressively across all my accounts, renewing those that are failing, because this model probably has a limited lifespan." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~10:41, L443); ("Or, if they do, you need to have a backup account so that your advantage can still be realized across all your accounts." — JJ, S8fSNbPFuLA ~6:30, L281). The Lucid sequence is the worked example of the cycle: ("And I did decide to stick with it, reinvest into four more. As you can see, I lost them within a week. So, it can be pretty bad." — JJ, C-_0QhKZmA4 ~8:28, L306). The poker framing: ("One account blowing is the same as losing one table. It doesn't matter because the math plays out across the sample." — JJ, nvOaN6mRu-o ~0:00, L23, written description). Bankroll discipline on the way up: ("With your fund management system, you can't deploy your entire stack at once. You have a higher probability of losing everything and you want your risk of ruin to be as low as possible." — JJ, xNeBEqv06k0 ~3:18, L146); ("you could also manage a couple of 150K accounts alongside your 50K accounts. So, there's no need to delete all your 50K accounts and only trade the 150K ones as soon as you start making money." — JJ, 4BXpI-hYqe0 ~9:00, L338).

*The held-back account.* The only "hedge" in the corpus is an account left out of a trade, not an opposing position: ("I took it for four accounts. Just wanted to leave one account uh in case I lost the trade." — JJ, RnwOqmEF6Rk ~0:24, L57; 2026-04-09, one of five FundedNext Advanced accounts, after the first trade had paid on all five). The same idea at firm level is the E8 reserve of passed evaluations (block above) and the Topstep live account he was willing to lose because ("I have five new assessments waiting for me to trade as soon as I finish" — JJ, pwO9IAu7FUQ ~13:42, L478).

*Platform and tooling.* Accounts sit on Tradovate: ("Let me just go to Tradeovate. This is where I have my accounts." — JJ, RnwOqmEF6Rk ~0:29, L60); ("Because I'm able to open literally 10 trade of eight tabs." — JJ, KHEQ5g55dQ4 ~1:02:14, L1975, medium; "trade of eight" is the caption for Tradovate); ("in TradiGate, there are tons." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~32:44, L1110, medium, followed by the firm list). In the April video Topstep, Tradovate and what the caption renders as "EA" (presumably E8) are named side by side as where the 150k accounts live: ("This is what I use on my 150k's, which would be obviously Topstep I only trade 150k's same EA same with Tradovate." — JJ, Lep139B5EbU ~2:56, L129). Two machines and a self-built dashboard: ("it's hard to record when I'm trading two different computers" — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~1:13, L66); ("basically have a control panel on my second monitor of all my accounts and it tells me that this account needs 50 points. This one needs 25. This one needs 70, simply based on the optimal risk I've simulated through millions of attempts." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~1:29, L79); ("it takes in all of this data and then it prints out the statistically optimal decision for the accounts moving forward uh versus the account state." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~22:52, L679); the inputs ("That is basically EVEL funded what's its balance and what's its draw down? Which firm is it on and what is the name of the plan? Like is it a flex, a direct, an instant funded, a growth, whatever like what is the plan? Then based on that, I would know what the payout rules are." — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~23:05, L685); ("so like I manually trade, but the automation does the optimal sizing" — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~25:39, L758); and a public calculator ("I'm going to plug into my website at the end and I'll show you exactly what Prop Firm is optimal for me." — JJ, MVP7X-3v8xk ~47:44, L1291). Copier: on 2026-07-30 ("This is my copier if I ever use one, which I don't yet." — JJ, AxP-cg50TdM ~32:35, L1105, medium). No copier product, TopstepX, Rithmic or Replikanto is named in any of the 250 facts. Order mechanics: ("I just had my stop loss here and then a buy limit right above here. So I can usually enter and exit really quickly on open, which is so useful." — JJ, PN1UKQMPb5M ~4:58, L178); overnight ("I had a lot of limit orders in the previous structure to sell while it was going down." — JJ, pwO9IAu7FUQ ~15:46, L546). Instrument error: ("first entering on NQ versus MNQ has probably lost me at least 100K" — JJ, dJdBnSBJlgQ ~12:52, L395). Payout rail: ("This is where most of the prop firms pay out." — JJ, 7UXiI2arAlQ ~0:10, L28; a Wise account per results.json L26). Why no live streams: ("I don't do live streams on YouTube because funding firms don't allow copy trading. They do not allow group or coordinated trading." — JJ, 7KJOh2NF-lQ ~32:17, L1093).

*Weekly routine.* The facts are thin. ("what I did was go to sleep around here, at 8:00 p.m." — JJ, pwO9IAu7FUQ ~15:46, L546) with resting limits in place; ("night trading is great for passing various assessments, trading funded accounts, and all that kind of stuff." — JJ, pwO9IAu7FUQ ~16:27, L568); a Thursday account check (HlWSP7ajgpQ L310, medium); a Friday burst of 35 evaluations (g68HHbrIBBg L779); on FOMC day ("there was like what one two three four five six seven trades. when I'm trying to get through 50 accounts." — JJ, l6iq0ljhxIo ~13:51, L470); daily payouts from FundedNext and lump sums from Apex (5RzMu2B2E_0 L216). The daily objective replaces a weekly plan: ("with prop firms I try to trade all of my accounts every single day." — JJ, aCOgfvL6lK8 ~10:25, L423). No fixed weekly schedule is stated anywhere in the group.
